Bicategories of algebras for relative pseudomonads Thanks: N. Arkor was supported by a departmental postdoctoral grant from the Department of Software Science at Tallinn University of Technology. P. Saville was supported by the Air Force Office of Scientific Research (award number FA9550-21-1-0038)
Nathanael Arkor, Philip Saville, Andrew Slattery

Abstract
. We introduce pseudoalgebras for relative pseudomonads and develop their theory. For each relative pseudomonad T T , we construct a free–forgetful relative pseudoadjunction that exhibits the bicategory of T T -pseudoalgebras as terminal among resolutions of T T . The Kleisli bicategory for T T thus embeds into the bicategory of pseudoalgebras as the sub-bicategory of free pseudoalgebras. We consequently obtain a coherence theorem that implies, for instance, that the bicategory of distributors is biequivalent to the 2-category of presheaf categories. In doing so, we extend several aspects of the theory of pseudomonads to relative pseudomonads, including doctrinal adjunction, transport of structure, and lax-idempotence. As an application of our general theory, we prove that, for each class of colimits Φ \Phi , there is a correspondence between monads relative to free Φ \Phi -cocompletions, and Φ \Phi -cocontinuous monads on free Φ \Phi -cocompletions.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
18C15, 18C20, 18D60, 18M50, 18N10, 18N15, 18N20

1.
Introduction

Two-dimensional monad theory, which is the abstract study of two-dimensional algebraic structure on categories (or, more generally, the objects of a bicategory), is one of the cornerstones of two-dimensional category theory. Many fundamental structures in category theory – such as monoidal categories, categories with a class of limits and/or colimits, and fibrations – may be captured as the algebras for pseudomonads
[
Str80
,
BKP89
,
Lac10
]
. The theory of two-dimensional monads and their algebras has been extensively developed (e.g.
[
Kel73
,
Kel74
,
Bun74
,
BKP89
,
Pow89
,
KL97
,
LMV02
,
Lac02
,
BG13
,
Bou14
,
Wal19
]
), providing a powerful framework to study such structures.

Relative pseudomonads

However, there are various important constructions in category theory that do not form pseudomonads yet are, in a certain sense, not far from doing so. A notable example is the presheaf construction, which associates to each small category
A
the category of presheaves on
A
, i.e. the functor category
[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
. The presheaf construction is ‘‘almost’’ a pseudomonad, except that the presheaf category is generally only locally small
1
1

1

The exception being when
A=0
, in which case
[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]\simeq 1
is the terminal category.
, and so the construction may not be iterated. This prompted
[
FGHW18
]
to introduce
relative pseudomonads

[
FGHW18
]
, a generalisation to two dimensions of the relative monads of
[
ACU10
]
. The notion of relative pseudomonad generalises that of a pseudomonad by relaxing the requirement that the underlying pseudofunctor be an endo-pseudofunctor. Thus, while the presheaf construction does not form a pseudomonad, it does give an example of a pseudomonad
relative
to the inclusion
\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
of small categories into locally small categories. Extending the classical setting of pseudomonads, the authors of
[
FGHW18
]
showed that to each
J
-relative pseudomonad
T
one may associate a
Kleisli bicategory

\mathrm{Kl}(T)
, whose 1-cells
X{\mathrel{\to}}Y
are precisely 1-cells
J(X)\to T(Y)
in the base. In particular, the Kleisli bicategory for the presheaf construction is precisely
[
Bén73
]
’s bicategory
{\mathbf{Dist}}
of small categories, distributors (a.k.a. profunctors or (bi)modules), and natural transformations
[
Bén73
]
.
2
2

2

This fact appears to have been first observed informally by
[
Gui75
]
in example 1.b) of
[
Gui75
, 47]
.
As explored in
[
FGHW18
]
and subsequent work, this perspective has useful consequences in a range of areas of mathematics and computer science, for instance in the theory of combinatorial species
[
FGHW08
]
, concurrency
[
CW05
]
, linear logic
[
Gal20
,
Oli21
]
, generalised operads and multicategories
[
CS10
,
Cur12
,
Hyl14
]
, and set theory
[
Lew20
]
.

Algebras for relative pseudomonads

While
[
FGHW18
]
develops a substantial portion of the theory of relative pseudomonads, a notable omission is any treatment of algebras. Algebras play a prominent role in two-dimensional monad theory: just as the algebras for a monad generalise the concept of algebraic structure on a set (such as monoids or rings), so algebras for a pseudomonad generalise the concept of algebraic structure on a category (such as monoidal or duoidal categories). It is therefore desirable to have a theory of algebras for relative pseudomonads, analogous to that in the one-dimensional setting
[
ACU15
]
. This is the purpose of the present paper.

We begin by defining the notion of a pseudoalgebra for a relative pseudomonad
T
(
Definition

3.1
) and constructing a bicategory of pseudoalgebras
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
(
Theorem

3.9
). In fact, in the spirit of two-dimensional algebra
[
BKP89
]
, we define several bicategories of pseudoalgebras, in which the
1-cells
are the strict, pseudo, lax, and colax morphisms of pseudoalgebras respectively. Pseudoalgebras for relative pseudomonads jointly generalise the algebras for relative 2-monads considered by
[
AM24
]
, and the pseudoalgebras for no-iteration pseudomonads considered by
[
MW13
]
.

We justify our definition by showing that various key results in the theory of pseudomonads extend to our setting. Often, though not always, the proof strategies in the relative setting are similar to those in the classical setting. However, care must be taken to rework statements and proofs to avoid iteration of the pseudomonad. We consider four aspects of the theory of pseudoalgebras in particular.

First, in
Section

3
we show that the bicategory of pseudoalgebras is part of a free–forgetful relative pseudoadjunction inducing
T
(
Section

3.3
), and take a step towards its characterisation as a three-dimensional limit (
Section

3.4
).

(1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Second, we study
doctrinal adjunction
, which is the study of structure-preserving adjunctions between pseudoalgebras
[
Kel74
]
. In
Section

4
we extend the main results of doctrinal adjunction to relative pseudomonads, and use it to establish
transport of structure
, which shows that pseudoalgebra structure may be transferred across equivalences – and, more generally, across (co)reflections.

Third, we study
lax-idempotence
for relative pseudomonads (
Section

5
). In comparison to the theory of lax-idempotent pseudomonads, which were introduced to capture pseudomonads exhibiting free cocompletions under classes of colimits
[
Koc95
,
Zöb76
]
, the relative setting is rather subtle. The authors of
[
FGHW18
]
proposed a definition of lax-idempotence for relative pseudomonads and showed that the presheaf construction is lax-idempotent in this sense. However, we show that this notion is too weak to capture theorems of interest. To rectify this, we introduce lax-idempotence for pseudoalgebras and use it to recover aspects of the theory known for non-relative pseudomonads. We show that a relative pseudomonad is lax-idempotent in the sense of
[
FGHW18
]
if and only if every
free
pseudoalgebra is lax-idempotent; in contrast to the non-relative setting
[
Koc95
]
, this is not enough to ensure every non-free pseudoalgebra is lax-idempotent.

Finally, we prove two-dimensional analogues of the well-known facts that, among adjunctions inducing a fixed monad, the Kleisli adjunction is initial and the Eilenberg–Moore adjunction is terminal
[
Kle65
,
EM65
]
. In
[
FGHW18
, Theorem 4.4]
, the Kleisli bicategory for a relative pseudomonad
T
was shown to be part of a relative pseudoadjunction inducing
T
.

(2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Kl}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

We show that, among relative pseudoadjunctions inducing
T
, (
2
) is bi-initial (
Theorem

6.3
) and (
1
) is 2-terminal (
Theorem

6.10
). As a consequence, there is a unique, fully faithful pseudofunctor
\mathrm{Kl}(T)\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
commuting with the left and right pseudoadjoints, whose image is the full sub-bicategory of free pseudoalgebras. While this result appears analogous to the one-dimensional setting, it has a useful corollary that only emerges in two dimensions. When the codomain of
T
is a 2-category,
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
is also a 2-category, whereas
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
need not be. Embedding of the Kleisli bicategory into the 2-category of pseudoalgebras thus exhibits a coherence theorem for
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
that captures many naturally occurring situations.

This brings us to our examples. As mentioned above, a motivating example of a relative pseudomonad is the presheaf construction. It is clear what the pseudoalgebras for the presheaf construction
ought
to be: namely, the locally small categories admitting small colimits. Perhaps surprisingly, establishing this is not straightforward – nevertheless, it is true, as we show in
Section

7
. As a consequence of our coherence theorem, we conclude that the bicategory of distributors is biequivalent to the full sub-2-category of the 2-category of cocomplete categories spanned by the presheaf categories (a result present in the categorical folklore, but for which, to our understanding, no rigorous proof has previously been given). In fact, we work more generally, and characterise the pseudoalgebras not just for the presheaf relative pseudomonad but for the relative pseudomonads exhibiting the free cocompletions of small categories under a class
\Phi
of colimits (
Theorem

7.14
).

We conclude the paper in
Section

8
with an application that demonstrates how the theory of relative
pseudomonads
sheds light on the theory of relative
monads
. In particular, by considering monads in the respective bicategories, we use the embedding of the Kleisli bicategory into the bicategory of pseudoalgebras to deduce that monads relative to free
\Phi
-cocompletions are equivalent to
\Phi
-cocontinuous monads on free
\Phi
-cocompletions.

Future directions

This paper is a first step towards two-dimensional relative monad theory. For reasons of space there are many topics we have not covered here. For instance, a key theme in the theory of 2-monads is the study of weak structures in terms of stricter structures (e.g. representing lax morphisms as certain strict morphisms
[
BKP89
]
). It would be useful to extend these ideas to the relative setting.

One of the motivations for our development is in connection to the theory of pseudocommutativity. In
[
Sla23
,
Sla24
]
, the third-named author initiated the study of pseudocommutativity for relative pseudomonads. In the one-dimensional setting, it is known that commutative monads equip their categories of algebras, under mild assumptions, with monoidal structure
[
Koc70
]
; aspects of this theory have recently been extended to the setting of monoidal pseudomonads
[
Mir24
]
. We expect something similar to be true for pseudocommutative relative pseudomonads, which in particular include all lax-idempotent relative pseudomonads. This would give, for instance, an abstract construction of the tensor product of categories equipped with a class of small colimits
[
Kel82
, §6.5]
.

Finally, in future work we intend to study the pseudoalgebras arising from distributive laws of relative pseudomonads, so as to capture a variety of structures in category theory that are sensitive to size
[
GU71
,
IK86
,
GL12
]
. Such distributive laws are also relevant to recent developments in the semantics of linear and modal logics
[
FGHW08
,
Oli21
,
Gal20
,
Kav24
,
Kav24a
]
, and it would be natural to consider the corresponding bicategories of pseudoalgebras as candidates for models of linear logic, in the style of call-by-name models or adjunction models
[
Lev03
,
CFM16
]
.

2.
Background

2.1.
Bicategories and pseudofunctors

We shall assume basic familiarity with two-dimensional category theory, including the definitions of 2-categories and bicategories, 2-functors and pseudofunctors, pseudonatural transformations, and modifications, as well as the theory of mates for adjunctions (see
[
JY21
]
for a general reference).

For the most part, our notation is chosen for consistency with that of
[
FGHW18
]
. We denote generic 2-categories and bicategories by calligraphic script, e.g.
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{C}}
; and their objects by uppercase Latin letters, e.g.
X,Y,Z
. We denote by
\mathbf{CAT}
the 2-category of locally small categories, functors, and natural transformations; and by
\mathbf{Cat}
the full sub-2-category spanned by the small categories. Given a bicategory
{\mathcal{A}}
and a pair of objects
X,Y\in{\mathcal{A}}
, we denote by
{\mathcal{A}}[X,Y]
the category of 1-cells from
X
to
Y
(denoted by lowercase Latin letters, e.g.
f,g,h
), and the 2-cells therebetween (denoted by lowercase Greek letters, e.g.
\phi,\gamma,\eta
). Given bicategories
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}
, we denote by
\mathbf{Bicat}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})
the bicategory of pseudofunctors from
{\mathcal{A}}
to
{\mathcal{B}}
(denoted by uppercase Latin letters, e.g.
F,G,H
), and the pseudonatural transformations and modifications therebetween; note that
\mathbf{Bicat}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})
is a 2-category if
{\mathcal{B}}
is. We denote the underlying collection of objects of a (bi)category
A
by
|A|
, and similarly write
|F|
for the underlying action on objects of a (pseudo)functor.

We shall denote the structural natural isomorphisms for associativity and unitality of composition in a bicategory by unnamed isomorphisms,

\displaystyle(hg)f

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}h(gf)

\displaystyle 1f

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}f

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}f1

and similarly for the compositor and unitor natural isomorphisms for a pseudofunctor. A pseudofunctor is
strict
when these isomorphisms are in fact identities.

\displaystyle F(g)F(f)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}F(gf)

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}F(1)

In pasting diagrams, we allow ourselves to elide the associator isomorphisms where it aids readability, and simply write, for instance,
hgf
; this is formally justified by the coherence theorem for bicategories
[
JY21
, §8.4]
. Similarly, diagrams that commute as a consequence of coherence for bicategories will generally not be explicitly labelled. We denote horizontal composition of 2-cells
\alpha
and
\beta
by
\beta\cdot\alpha
. We shall employ a diagrammatic convention in which rounded arrows on the outside of a diagram indicate definitional equality. For instance, if
h\mathrel{:=}g\circ f
, we may write the following. This makes it easier to distinguish the non-trivial equalities in a commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

Finally, in an adjunction, we refer to the triangle law
\ell\Rightarrow\ell r\ell\Rightarrow\ell
as the
left triangle law
, and
r\Rightarrow r\ell r\Rightarrow r
as the
right triangle law
.

2.2.
Relative pseudoadjunctions

We now recall the fundamental concepts introduced in
[
FGHW18
]
. The central notions are that of a
relative pseudoadjunction
and of a
relative pseudomonad
.
A relative pseudoadjunction is a joint generalisation of the notion of relative adjunction
[
Ulm68
,
ACU15
]
and the notion of pseudoadjunction
[
Str80
]
.

Definition 2.1

(
[
FGHW18
, Definition 3.6]
, cf.
[
Prz14
, Definition 2.12]
)
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor between bicategories. A
J
-relative pseudoadjunction
comprises

(2.1.(1))

a pseudofunctor
R\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
;

(2.1.(2))

an object
LX\in{\mathcal{C}}
for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
;

(2.1.(3))

a 1-cell
i_{X}\colon JX\to RLX
in
{\mathcal{E}}
for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
;

(2.1.(4))

an adjoint equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RY]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}[LX,Y]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{\sharp}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{LX,Y}\!{({-})}\circ i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
Y\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

Recall that the presheaf construction sends a small category
X
to the locally small presheaf category
[X{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
. This assignment has a universal property: it is the free cocompletion of
X
under small colimits
[
And66
]
. However, note that this universal property identifies the free cocompletion up to equivalence rather than up to isomorphism: thus, it is properly described as a bicategorical universal property. Furthermore, since the presheaf construction takes small categories to locally small categories, it does not exhibit a pseudoadjunction, but rather a relative pseudoadjunction (while the presheaf construction is defined also on large categories, it only satisfies the universal property on the small categories: see
Example

2.8
). The presheaf construction thus provides one of the motivating examples of a relative pseudoadjunction
[
FGHW18
]
.

Definition 2.2
.

Denote by
\mathbf{COC}
the 2-category whose objects are locally small categories equipped with a choice of small colimits: specifically, an object is a locally small category
A
along with, for each small category
D
and functor
f\colon D\to A
, a cocone under
f
that is colimiting. A 1-cell is a functor that sends the colimiting cocones to colimiting cocones (not necessarily the chosen ones). The 2-cells are natural transformations.

Example 2.3

(
[
FGHW18
, Example 3.9]
)
.

Denote by
J\colon\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
the inclusion of the
2-category
of small categories into the 2-category of locally small categories. The presheaf construction
X\mapsto[X{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
exhibits a left
J
-relative pseudoadjoint
P\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{COC}
to the forgetful 2-functor
U\colon\mathbf{COC}\to\mathbf{CAT}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{COC}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CAT}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The unit
y_{X}\colon X\to[X{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
is given by the Yoneda embedding. For each small category
X
and cocomplete locally small category
A
, the functor

\mathbf{CAT}[X,U(A)]\xleftarrow{U{({-})}\circ y_{X}}\mathbf{COC}[P(X),A]

admits a left adjoint equivalence, given by left extension along
y_{A}
.

Example 2.4
.

When
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is a functor between categories (viewed as a 2-functor between locally discrete 2-categories), a
J
-relative pseudoadjunction is the same as a
J
-relative adjunction
[
Ulm68
, Definition 2.2]
.

Example 2.5
.

When
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is a 2-functor between 2-categories and the adjoint equivalences appearing in
Definition

2.1
are isomorphisms of categories, we obtain the notion of
relative 2-adjunction
, i.e. a
\mathbf{CAT}
-enriched relative adjunction
[
AM24
, Example 8.11]
.

For every relative pseudoadjunction as in
Definition

2.1
, it follows from
[
FGHW18
, Lemma 3.7]
that the object assignment
L
extends essentially uniquely to a pseudofunctor such that the family
\{i_{X}\colon JX\to RLX\}_{X\in{\mathcal{A}}}
may be equipped with the structure of a pseudonatural transformation. The action of the pseudofunctor
L
at
X,Y\in{\mathcal{A}}
is given by the following composite.

While it is not proven ibid., it also follows that
\sharp
is pseudonatural with respect to this choice; the following generalises
[
Fio06
, Lemmas 9.14 & 9.15]
from pseudoadjunctions to relative pseudoadjunctions.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
(L,R,i,\sharp,\eta,\varepsilon)
be a
J
-relative pseudoadjunction as in
Definition

2.1
. The family of functors

\big\{\sharp_{X,Y}\colon{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RY]\to{\mathcal{C}}[LX,Y]\big\}_{X\in{\mathcal{A}},Y\in{\mathcal{C}}}

admits the structure of a pseudonatural equivalence
\sharp\colon{\mathcal{E}}[J{-}_{1},R{-}_{2}]\Rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}[L{-}_{1},{-}_{2}]\colon{\mathcal{A}}{}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbf{Cat}
.

Proof.

The following composite admits a canonical pseudonatural transformation structure,

{\mathcal{C}}[L{-}_{1},{-}_{2}]\xrightarrow{R_{L{-}_{1},{-}_{2}}}{\mathcal{E}}[RL{-}_{1},R{-}_{2}]\xrightarrow{{\mathcal{E}}[i{-}_{1},R{-}_{2}]}{\mathcal{E}}[J{-}_{1},R{-}_{2}]

since both postcomposition and
i
are pseudonatural
[
Sav19
,
FGHW18
]
. Furthermore, since each of the components of the pseudonatural transformation are adjoint equivalences, the family of adjoints to the components is canonically equipped with the structure of a pseudonatural transformation in the opposite direction
[
Sav19
, Lemma 2.1.16]
. Consequently,
\sharp
is pseudonatural.
∎

2.3.
Relative pseudomonads

A relative pseudomonad is a joint generalisation of the notion of relative monad
[
ACU10
,
ACU15
]
and the notion of pseudomonad
[
Bun74
]
.

Definition 2.7

(
[
FGHW18
, Definition 3.1]
)
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor between bicategories. A
J
-relative pseudomonad
comprises

(2.7.(1))

an object
TX\in{\mathcal{E}}
for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
;

(2.7.(2))

a functor
{({-})}^{*}_{X,Y}\colon{\mathcal{E}}[JX,TY]\to{\mathcal{E}}[TX,TY]
for each
X,Y\in{\mathcal{A}}
, the
extension operator
(we allow ourselves to elide the subscripts where they may be inferred from context);

(2.7.(3))

a 1-cell
i_{X}\colon JX\to TX
for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
, the
unit
;

(2.7.(4))

a natural family of invertible 2-cells
\mu_{g,f}\colon(g^{*}f)^{*}\Rightarrow g^{*}f^{*}
for each
f\colon JX\to TY
and
g\colon JY\to TZ
in
{\mathcal{E}}
;

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g^{*}f)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{g,f}}

(2.7.(5))

a natural family of invertible 2-cells
\eta_{f}\colon f\Rightarrow f^{*}i_{X}
for each
f\colon JX\to TY
in
{\mathcal{E}}
;

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces JX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{f}}

(2.7.(6))

a family of invertible 2-cells
\theta_{X}\colon(i_{X})^{*}\Rightarrow 1_{TX}
for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
,

such that

(2.7.(7))

for each
f\colon JW\to TX
,
g\colon JX\to TY
, and
h\colon JY\to TZ
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the following associativity law holds;

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((h^{*}g)^{*}f)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{*}g)^{*}f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((h^{*}g^{*})f)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{*}g^{*})f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{*}(g^{*}f))^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{*}(g^{*}f)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{*}(g^{*}f^{*})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{h^{*}g,f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{h,g}f^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mu_{h,g}f)^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{h,g^{*}f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{*}\mu_{g,f}}}

(2.7.(8))

for each
f\colon JX\to TY
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the following unit law holds.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{*}i_{X})^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}{(i_{X})}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}1_{TX}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\eta_{f})^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{f,i_{X}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\theta_{X}}

By
[
FGHW18
, Theorem 3.8]
, each relative pseudoadjunction
(L,R,i,\sharp,\eta,\varepsilon)
induces a relative pseudomonad whose underlying pseudofunctor is given by
RL
, whose extension operator is given by

(4)

{({-})}^{*}_{X,Y}\mathrel{:=}{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RLY]\xrightarrow{{({-})}^{\sharp}_{X,LY}}{\mathcal{E}}[LX,LY]\xrightarrow{R_{LX,LY}}{\mathcal{E}}[RLX,RLY]

and whose unit is given by
i
.

Example 2.8
.

The relative pseudoadjunction of
Example

2.3
induces a
(\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT})
-relative pseudomonad, which sends a small category
X
to the presheaf category
P(X)\mathrel{:=}[X{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
. Note that relativity is crucial in this example: although the presheaf category
[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
is defined for arbitrary large categories
A
, the unit
y_{A}\colon A\to[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
is defined only when
A
is locally small, and the requisite left extensions defining the extension operator exist generally only when
A
is small. (However, there is a closely related construction – the
small presheaf
construction – which does define a pseudomonad on
\mathbf{CAT}
; we discuss its relation to the presheaf construction in
Section

7
.)

Example 2.9
.

When
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is a functor between categories (viewed as a 2-functor between locally discrete 2-categories), a
J
-relative pseudomonad is the same as a
J
-relative monad
[
ACU15
, Definition 2.1]
.

Example 2.10
.

When
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is a 2-functor between 2-categories and each of the families
\mu
,
\eta
, and
\theta
appearing in
Definition

2.1
are identities, we obtain the notion of
relative 2-monad
, i.e. a
\mathbf{CAT}
-enriched relative monad
[
AM24
, Example 8.14]
.

For every relative pseudomonad as in
Definition

2.7
, it follows from
[
FGHW18
, Proposition 4.7]
that the object assignment
T
extends to a pseudofunctor, where the action on a 1-cell
{f\colon X\to Y}
in
{\mathcal{A}}
is given by
(i_{Y}\circ Jf)^{*}\colon TX\to TY
, and that the families
\{i_{X}\colon JX\to TX\}_{X\in{\mathcal{A}}}
and
\{{({-})}^{*}_{X,Y}\colon{\mathcal{E}}[J{-}_{1},T{-}_{2}]\to{\mathcal{E}}[T{-}_{1},T{-}_{2}]\}_{X,Y\in{\mathcal{A}}}
may consequently be equipped with the structure of pseudonatural transformations.
3
3

3

[
FGHW18
]
do not show that the families of invertible 2-cells
\mu
,
\eta
, and
\theta
become modifications under these assignments. That
\eta
forms a modification will follow from
Lemma

3.22
, and that
\theta
forms a modification is trivial. However, to prove that
\mu
forms a modification would require a detour into the theory of pseudodistributors, which we prefer to avoid here.

2.4.
The Kleisli bicategory

To every
(J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}})
-relative pseudomonad
T
, we may associate a bicategory with the same objects as
{\mathcal{A}}
, whose 1-cells from
X
to
Y
are given by 1-cells
JX\to TY
in
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Definition 2.11

(
[
FGHW18
, Theorem 4.1]
)
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor and let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad. The
Kleisli bicategory
of
T
has the same objects as
{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathrm{Kl}(T)[X,Y]\mathrel{:=}{\mathcal{E}}[JX,TY]
. The identity on an object
X\in\mathrm{Kl}(T)
is given by
i_{X}\colon JX\to TX
, and the composite of 1-cells
f\colon JX\to TY
and
g\colon JY\to TZ
is given by
g^{*}\circ f\colon JX\to TZ
.

Recall that a distributor (a.k.a. profunctor) from
X
to
Y
is a functor
Y{}^{\mathrm{op}}\times X\to{\mathbf{Set}}

[
Bén73
]
. Small categories, distributors, and natural transformations assemble into a bicategory
{\mathbf{Dist}}
(a.k.a.
\mathbf{Prof}
). One of the main contributions of
[
FGHW18
]
was to give an abstract construction of the bicategory
{\mathbf{Dist}}
.

Example 2.12

(
[
FGHW18
, Example 4.2]
)
.

Let
P
be the
(\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT})
-relative pseudomonad of
Example

2.8
. Then
\mathrm{Kl}(P)
is the bicategory
{\mathbf{Dist}}
.

Just as in the one-dimensional setting, the Kleisli bicategory exhibits every relative pseudomonad as induced by a relative pseudoadjunction.

Theorem 2.13

(
[
FGHW18
, Lemma 3.4, Theorem 4.4]
)
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor. Every
J
-relative pseudomonad
T
is induced by a relative pseudoadjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Kl}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

in which
K_{T}
is identity-on-objects, and
V_{T}
is given on objects by the action of
T
and on morphisms by the extension operator
{({-})}^{*}
.

We will show in
Section

6
that this relative pseudoadjunction is universal in an appropriate sense.

3.
Pseudoalgebras for relative pseudomonads

We now turn to the central notion of this paper: that of a pseudoalgebra for a relative pseudomonad. While relative pseudomonads describe what it means to freely adjoin coherent structure to an object of a bicategory, a pseudoalgebra describes what it means for an object to be equipped with such structure, not necessarily freely. For instance, we shall prove in
Section

7
that the pseudoalgebras for the presheaf construction of
Example

2.8
are precisely the locally small categories with a choice of small colimits.

We begin in
Section

3.1
by introducing the definitions of pseudoalgebras, their (strict, pseudo, lax, and colax) morphisms, and transformations, and establishing that they form bicategories (
Theorem

3.9
). Next, we show in
Section

3.2
that this definition is a faithful generalisation of the bicategories of pseudoalgebras for non-relative pseudomonads by establishing an equivalence between pseudoalgebras for pseudomonads relative to the identity, and pseudoalgebras for no-iteration pseudomonads in the sense of
[
MW13
]
. In
Section

3.3
, we show that the construction of free
T
-pseudoalgebras exhibits a free–forgetful relative pseudoadjunction. Finally, in
Section

3.4
, we give a three-dimensional perspective on the bicategories of pseudoalgebras by showing that they admit strict pseudofunctors into certain three-dimensional limits.

3.1.
Bicategories of pseudoalgebras

Algebras for relative monads were first studied by
[
Wal69
]
as the algebras for
devices

[
Wal69
,
Wal70
]
. They were introduced in full generality in
[
ACU10
]
. Our definition is a joint generalisation of the definition of an algebra for a relative 2-monad and of an algebra for a no-iteration pseudomonad
[
AM24
,
MW13
]
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor between bicategories.
A
pseudoalgebra
(or simply
T
-pseudoalgebra
) for a
J
-relative pseudomonad
T
as in
Definition

2.7
comprises

(3.1.(1))

an object
A\in{\mathcal{E}}
;

(3.1.(2))

a functor
{({-})}^{a}_{X}\colon{\mathcal{E}}[JX,A]\to{\mathcal{E}}[TX,A]
for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
, the
extension operator
(we allow ourselves to elide the subscript when it can be inferred from context);

(3.1.(3))

a natural family of invertible 2-cells
\hat{a}_{g,f}\colon(g^{a}f)^{a}\Rightarrow g^{a}f^{*}
for each
f\colon JX\to TY
and
g\colon JY\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
;

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g^{a}f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{g,f}}

(3.1.(4))

a natural family of invertible 2-cells
\tilde{a}_{f}\colon f\Rightarrow f^{a}i_{X}
for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces JX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{f}}

such that

(3.1.(5))

for each
f\colon JX\to TY
,
g\colon JY\to TZ
, and
h\colon JZ\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the following associativity law holds;

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((h^{a}g)^{a}f)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{a}g)^{a}f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((h^{a}g^{*})f)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{a}g^{*})f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{a}(g^{*}f))^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{a}(g^{*}f)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{a}(g^{*}f^{*})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{h^{a}g,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{h,g}f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\hat{a}_{h,g}f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{h,g^{*}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{a}\mu_{g,f}}

(3.1.(6))

for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the following diagram unit law holds.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}i_{X})^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}{(i_{X})}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}1_{TX}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{a}_{f})^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{f,i_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{a}\theta_{X}}

A pseudoalgebra is
strict
(or is a
strict algebra
) if each
\tilde{a}_{f}
and each
\hat{a}_{f,g}
is an identity 2-cell.

For
J
a pseudofunctor between 2-categories, pseudoalgebras for
J
-relative pseudomonads were introduced by
[
Lew20
, Definition 4.34]
; our definition coincides in that setting. (
\Citeauthor
lewicki2020categories does not define morphisms or transformations of pseudoalgebras.)

Example 3.2
.

Let
A
be a locally small category with a choice of small colimits (in the sense of
Definition

2.2
). Then
A
may be equipped with the structure of a
P
-pseudoalgebra. For each functor
f\colon JX\to A
, one defines
f^{a}
to be the pointwise left extension
(y_{X}\mathbin{\rhd}f)\colon PX\to A
of
f
along
y_{A}
, which sends each
p\in PX
to the colimit of
(f\circ\pi)\colon{\mathrm{El}}(p)\to X\to A
, where
{\mathrm{El}}(p)
is the category of elements associated to
p
. The natural families of invertible 2-cells are both induced by the universal properties of the left extensions. It is straightforward to verify directly that this structure does indeed satisfy the axioms of a pseudoalgebra, though we will give a more abstract proof in
Section

7
.

Example 3.3
.

When
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is a functor between categories and
T
is a
J
-relative monad (
Example

2.9
), a
T
-pseudoalgebra is the same as an
EM-algebra
in the sense of
[
ACU15
, Definition 2.11]
.

Remark 3.4
.

Definition

3.1
introduces the notions of both strict algebras and pseudoalgebras. One could also define the notions of
lax
and
colax algebras
for a relative pseudomonad. In fact, historically, two-dimensional notions of algebras were first considered by
[
Bun74
]
, who introduced lax algebras for
lax monads
(a generalisation of pseudomonads in which the structural natural isomorphisms are weakened to non-invertible natural transformations). In this paper, we shall only consider laxity for morphisms, not for pseudomonads or pseudoalgebras. There are several reasons for this choice. First, the theory in
[
FGHW18
]
was developed only in the context of relative pseudomonads, and it would therefore be necessary first to revisit this development in the more general context of relative (co)lax monads to obtain the appropriate generalisations of the theorems herein (necessitating lax notions of two-dimensional relative adjunctions, Kleisli lax bicategories, and so on). Second, definitions in the fully lax setting are more complicated, requiring additional coherence axioms (see
Lemma

3.15
and the discussion preceding it). Third, the existing literature on two-dimensional monad theory focuses primarily on pseudo and strict algebras. Fourth, and most importantly, we currently have no motivating examples of (co)lax algebras in the relative setting. Nonetheless, we do expect our proofs to generalise to the lax setting.

As in the non-relative setting, there are several notions of morphism between pseudoalgebras.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor.
A
lax morphism
from a
T
-pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
to a
T
-pseudoalgebra
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
comprises

(3.5.(1))

a morphism
h\colon A\to B
in
{\mathcal{E}}
;

(3.5.(2))

a natural family of 2-cells
\overline{h}_{f}\colon(hf)^{b}\Rightarrow hf^{a}
for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
,

such that

(3.5.(3))

for each
f\colon JX\to TY
and
g\colon JY\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the following associativity law holds;

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((hg)^{b}f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hg)^{b}f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((hg^{a})f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hg^{a})f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h(g^{a}f))^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(g^{a}f)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(g^{a}f^{*})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{b}_{hg,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{g}f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{h}_{g}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{g^{a}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\hat{a}_{g,f}}

(3.5.(4))

for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the following unit law holds.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces hf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hf)^{b}i_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hf^{a})i_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(f^{a}i_{X})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}_{hf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{f}i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\tilde{a}_{f}}

(h,\overline{h})
is a
pseudomorphism
if each
\overline{h}_{f}
is invertible; and is a
strict morphism
if each
\overline{h}_{f}
is an identity 2-cell. A
colax morphism
is defined analogously, except that the direction of each
\overline{h}
is reversed, hence involves a natural family of 2-cells
\overline{h}_{f}\colon hf^{a}\Rightarrow(hf)^{b}
for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Example 3.6
.

In the context of
Example

3.2
, every functor
f\colon A\to B
between locally small categories may be equipped with the structure of a lax morphism of
P
-pseudoalgebras, the natural family of 2-cells being induced by the universal property of the left extensions. (This is a special property of the relative pseudomonad
P
, which we examine further in
Sections

5
and
7
; in general, it is not true that, for a relative pseudomonad
T
, every 1-cell in
{\mathcal{E}}
admits the structure of a lax morphism.) If
f
preserves small colimits, the lax morphism is furthermore a pseudomorphism.

There is a single notion of 2-cell between morphisms of pseudoalgebras (modulo the direction of the data involved in a lax/colax morphism).

Definition 3.7
.

Let
(h,\overline{h})
and
(h^{\prime},\overline{h^{\prime}})
be lax morphisms from
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
to
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
. A
transformation
from
(h,\overline{h})
to
(h^{\prime},\overline{h^{\prime}})
is a 2-cell
\alpha\colon h\Rightarrow h^{\prime}
rendering the following diagram commutative for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hf)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{\prime}f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hf^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{\prime}f^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h^{\prime}}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha f^{a}}

Transformations between colax morphisms are defined in the same way, modulo reversing the horizontal arrows above.

Example 3.8
.

In the context of
Examples

3.2
and
3.6
, every natural transformation between (cocontinuous) functors is a transformation of
P
-pseudoalgebras. Intuitively, this is because every natural transformation automatically coheres with colimit structure.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor and let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad. The
T
-pseudoalgebras
(
Definition

3.1
), lax morphisms (
Definition

3.5
), and transformations (
Definition

3.7
) form a bicategory
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
, the
bicategory of
T
-pseudoalgebras and lax morphisms
.

Proof.

We first show that, for any two pseudoalgebras
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
and
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
, the lax morphisms from
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
to
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
, together with their transformations, form a category. Since transformations are 2-cells in the underlying bicategory
{\mathcal{E}}
satisfying a compatibility property, it suffices to show that this property is preserved by identities and composition in the hom-category
{\mathcal{E}}[A,B]
. This is indeed true: for lax morphisms
h,h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}\colon(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})\to(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
, and transformations
\alpha\colon h\Rightarrow h^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}\colon h^{\prime}\Rightarrow h^{\prime\prime}
, the following diagrams commute by functoriality of
{({-})}^{b}
, exhibiting
1_{h}\colon h\Rightarrow h
and
\alpha^{\prime}\alpha\colon h\Rightarrow h^{\prime\prime}
as transformations.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hf)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hf)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hf^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hf^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{h}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{h}f^{a}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hf)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{\prime}f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(h^{\prime\prime}f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hf^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{\prime}f^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h^{\prime\prime}f^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h^{\prime}}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha f^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha^{\prime}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h^{\prime\prime}}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}f^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}\alpha f^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha f)^{b}}

Next, we observe that, for each

(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
, the family of 2-cells,

(5)

for each 1-cell

Consequently, the composition functor
{\circ}\colon{\mathcal{E}}[B,C]\times{\mathcal{E}}[A,B]\to{\mathcal{E}}[A,C]
lifts to a functor

\displaystyle{\circ}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)[(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b}),

\displaystyle(C,{-}^{c},\hat{c},\tilde{c})]\times\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)[(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a}),(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})]

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\longrightarrow\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)[(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a}),(C,{-}^{c},\hat{c},\tilde{c})]

on hom-categories of lax morphisms and transformations.

The associators and unitors for
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
are inherited from
{\mathcal{E}}
: it thus remains to show that these are transformations componentwise. This follows from the commutativity of the following three diagrams, for the unitors and associator respectively.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((h1_{A})f)^{b}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(((fg)h)k\right)^{d}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\left((gh)k^{a}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left((fg)h\right)k^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\left(f(gh)\right)k^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{fg}_{hk}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{(gh)k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{g(hk)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\overline{gh}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\overline{g}_{hk}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\overline{g}_{hk}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\overline{f}}

∎

Proposition 3.10
.

By restricting the 1-cells of
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
, we obtain:

(3.10.(1))

a bicategory
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
of
T
-pseudoalgebras and pseudomorphisms
, equipped with a locally fully faithful strict pseudofunctor
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)\to\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
;

(3.10.(2))

assuming
{\mathcal{E}}
is a 2-category, a 2-category
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{s}(T)
of
T
-pseudoalgebras and strict morphisms
, equipped with a locally fully faithful strict 2-functor
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{s}(T)\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
.

Furthermore, by considering instead
colax
morphisms in
Theorem

3.9
, we obtain

(3.10.(3))

a bicategory
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{c}(T)
of
T
-pseudoalgebras and colax morphisms
, together with a locally fully faithful strict pseudofunctor
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)\to\mathrm{PsAlg}_{c}(T)
.

By restricting each of the above to strict algebras, we obtain full sub-bicategories
\mathrm{Alg}_{l}(T)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
,
\mathrm{Alg}(T)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
,
\mathrm{Alg}_{c}(T)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PsAlg}_{c}(T)
; and, assuming
{\mathcal{E}}
is a 2-category, a 2-category
\mathrm{Alg}_{s}(T)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PsAlg}_{s}(T)
.

Proof.

For (1), we only need check that pseudomorphisms are closed under composition, since identities are pseudomorphisms by definition. However, this is trivial, since each of the components in (
6
) is then invertible. (2) is similar, except that for identities and composites to be strict, we furthermore require the structural isomorphisms in
{\mathcal{E}}
to be identities, so that (
5
) and (
6
) are identities. (3) follows by duality.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

When
{\mathcal{E}}
is a 2-category, each of the bicategories of pseudoalgebras in
Theorems

3.9
and
3.10
is a 2-category, since the structural isomorphisms are inherited from
{\mathcal{E}}
. This is in contrast to
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
, which is typically a bicategory when
T
is not a 2-monad, even when
{\mathcal{E}}
is a 2-category. This observation will play an important role in
Section

6
.

Example 3.12
.

Following
Remark

3.11
, when
J
is a 2-functor between 2-categories and
T
is a relative 2-monad,
\mathrm{Alg}_{s}(T)
is the 2-category of
\mathbf{Cat}
-enriched algebras in the sense of
[
AM24
]
.

For the next proposition, recall that an amnestic isofibration is a functor
u\colon C\to D
with the property that, for every isomorphism
\phi\colon d\to u(c)
in
D
there exists a unique isomorphism
\phi^{\prime}\colon d^{\prime}\to c
in
C
such that
u(\phi^{\prime})=\phi

[
Ave17
, Lemma 2.4.7]
.

Definition 3.14
.

We also denote by
U_{T}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)\to{\mathcal{E}}
the locally faithful, locally conservative, locally amnestic isofibrant, strict pseudofunctor
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)\to\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)\to{\mathcal{E}}
given by composing the pseudofunctors defined in
Propositions

3.10
and
3.13
, and similarly for the other variants.

3.2.
Comparison with no-iteration pseudoalgebras

To justify our definitions it is necessary to show that, when
J
is the identity pseudofunctor, we recover the usual notions of pseudoalgebra and their morphisms and transformations. We therefore compare our definitions to the corresponding notions for no-iteration pseudomonads. No-iteration pseudomonads were introduced in
[
MW13
]
as an alternative presentation of pseudomonads in terms of an extension operator
{\mathcal{E}}[{-}_{1},T{-}_{2}]\Rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}[T{-}_{1},T{-}_{2}]
rather than a multiplication operator
TT\Rightarrow T
. As shown in
[
FGHW18
, Proposition 3.3]
, these are precisely pseudomonads relative to the identity pseudofunctor. Here, we shall show that this relationship extends to the bicategories of pseudoalgebras.

We first establish a lemma that will be useful in later calculations. Conceptually, this corresponds to the additional axiom that would need to be imposed in the definition of a lax algebra for a relative pseudomonad (see
Remark

3.4
). However, for pseudoalgebras it is automatic.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
be a pseudoalgebra for a
(J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}})
-relative pseudomonad

. For each

and

in

, the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{a}f}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{a}(f^{*}i_{X})}}

Proposition 3.16
.

Under the identification of pseudomonads relative to the identity and no-iteration pseudomonads established in
[
FGHW18
, Proposition 3.3]
:

(3.16.(1))

a pseudoalgebra for an identity-relative pseudomonad in the sense of
Definition

3.1
is the same as a no-iteration pseudoalgebra;

(3.16.(2))

a pseudomorphism between pseudoalgebras in the sense of
Definition

3.5
is the same as a pseudomorphism between no-iteration pseudoalgebra;

(3.16.(3))

a transformation in the sense of
Definition

3.7
is the same as a transformation between pseudomorphisms of no-iteration pseudoalgebras.

Proof.

In each instance, the two notions involve exactly the same data, except for the direction of the invertible 2-cells.

(3.16.(1))

Using the numbering of axioms for a no-iteration pseudoalgebra in
[
MW13
, §4]
, the equivalences between the axioms for a relative pseudomonad pseudoalgebra and those for a no-iteration pseudoalgebra are given as follows.

\iff

Axioms 4 and 5

\tilde{a}

\iff

3.15

\iff

Axiom 3

Note that it follows that Axiom 3 for a no-iteration pseudoalgebras in

is redundant, in that it can be derived from the others. This was also observed in
[

, Corollary 6.4]
.

(3.16.(2))

Corollary 3.17
.

Under the identification of pseudomonads relative to the identity and no-iteration pseudomonads established in
[
FGHW18
, Proposition 3.3]
, the 2-category of pseudoalgebras and pseudomorphisms for an identity-relative pseudomonad on a 2-category (
Theorem

3.9
) is isomorphic to the 2-category of no-iteration pseudoalgebras in
[
MW13
, §4]
.

Proof.

Follows directly from
Proposition

3.16
, observing that identities and composition is defined in
[
MW13
, §4]
in the same way as in
Theorem

3.9
.
∎

Consequently, when
J
is the identity, it follows from
[
MW13
, Theorem 5.1]
that the bicategory of pseudoalgebras is biequivalent to the usual bicategory of pseudoalgebras for a pseudomonad in the classical sense
[
Bun74
, Definition 2.4]
.
4
4

4

[
MW13
]
consider no-iteration pseudomonads only on 2-categories, but the proof extends without fuss to bicategories.

Remark 3.18
.

Let us sketch the correspondence between pseudoalgebras for identity-relative pseudomonads, and pseudoalgebras for pseudomonads; the idea is the same as in the relationship between identity-relative pseudomonads and pseudomonads
[
FGHW18
, Remarks 3.4 & 3.5]
. Given an identity-relative pseudomonad, each pseudoalgebra
A
is equipped with a 1-cell
(1_{A})^{a}\colon TA\to A
. Conversely, given a pseudomonad, each pseudoalgebra
A
is equipped with an extension operator taking
f\colon X\to A
to
(a\circ Tf)\colon TX\to TA\to A
. The two families of invertible 2-cells are obtained in the evident way.

Given a lax morphism of pseudoalgebras for an identity-relative pseudomonad,
\overline{h}_{1_{A}}\colon h^{b}\Rightarrow h(1_{A})^{a}
exhibits a 2-cell as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hi_{B})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{A})^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{A})^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{1_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

This defines a lax morphism of pseudoalgebras for a pseudomonad in the usual sense.

Conversely, given a lax morphism
(h,\upsilon)
in the usual sense, for each 1-cell
f\colon X\to A
the following 2-cell exhibits a natural family of 2-cells defining a lax morphism in the sense of
Definition

3.5
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(hf)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Th}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

We note that, while
[
MW13
]
only consider
pseudo
morphisms, the above assignments extend to a correspondence between lax morphisms for no-iteration pseudoalgebras and lax morphisms for pseudoalgebras. The correspondence for pseudo, strict, and colax morphisms is obtained analogously.

3.3.
The free–forgetful relative pseudoadjunction

We now study the construction of free pseudoalgebras, and show that this construction exhibits a left relative pseudoadjoint to the forgetful functor
U_{T}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)\to{\mathcal{E}}
of
Proposition

3.13
. In
Section

6
, we will show that this relative pseudoadjunction is universal in an appropriate sense.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor, let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad, and let
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
. The object
TX
admits the structure of a
T
-pseudoalgebra, the
free
T
-pseudoalgebra on
X
.

Proof.

The extension operator is given by the family
{({-})}^{*}_{{-},X}\colon{\mathcal{E}}[J{-},TX]\to{\mathcal{E}}[T{-},TX]
; the natural families are given by
\mu
and
\eta
respectively. The pseudoalgebra axioms are then exactly the axioms of a relative pseudomonad.
∎

It is a recurring pattern in the theory of relative pseudomonads that properties of a relative pseudomonad will follow from corresponding properties of their free pseudoalgebras, since the data of a relative pseudomonad is essentially an axiomatisation of the notion of free pseudoalgebra. Moreover, the proofs establishing that properties hold for arbitrary pseudoalgebras often have the same structure as proofs of properties for relative pseudomonads qua free pseudoalgebras. In these cases, we will generally defer proofs to
[
FGHW18
]
, as it is unenlightening to reproduce the details here.

Given a 1-cell
f\colon JX\to A
into a
T
-pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
, the extension
f^{a}\colon TX\to A
is a 1-cell between
T
-pseudoalgebras. As expected, it is automatically structure-preserving.

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad and let
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
be a
T
-pseudoalgebra. For each object
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
and 1-cell
h\colon JX\to A
, the 1-cell
h^{a}\colon TX\to A
admits the structure of a pseudomorphism from the free
T
-pseudoalgebra on
X
. Furthermore, given any 2-cell
\alpha\colon h\Rightarrow h^{\prime}\colon JX\to A
, the 2-cell
\alpha^{a}\colon h^{a}\Rightarrow(h^{\prime})^{a}
is a transformation.

Proof.

The pseudomorphism structure on
h^{a}
is defined by

\overline{h^{a}}_{g}\mathrel{:=}\hat{a}_{h,g}\colon(h^{a}g)^{a}\to h^{a}g^{*}

and the two coherence conditions are precisely (
3.1.(5)
) and
Lemma

3.15
. The algebra transformation axiom for
\alpha^{a}
follows immediately from the naturality of
\hat{b}
.
∎

We are now in a position to justify our claim that

3.4.
A three-dimensional perspective on the bicategory of pseudoalgebras

As recalled in
Section

2.3
, while the definition of relative pseudomonad (
Definition

2.7
) is presented solely in terms of an assignment
X\mapsto TX
on objects, this assignment extends to a pseudofunctor. Furthermore, the families
i_{X}\colon JX\to TX
and
{({-})}^{*}_{X,Y}\colon{\mathcal{E}}[JX,TY]\to{\mathcal{E}}[TX,TY]
extend to pseudonatural transformations. In this subsection, we show something similar holds for pseudoalgebras. First, we show in
Lemma

3.22
that the extension operator for each pseudoalgebra is automatically pseudonatural, and that the unitors form modifications. Next, we examine the global structure formed by the pseudoalgebras for a fixed relative pseudomonad. In particular, we show in
Proposition

3.25
that each of the bicategories of pseudoalgebras defined in
Theorems

3.9
and
3.10
admit a canonical locally faithful strict pseudofunctor into a bicategory whose objects are pairs of an object
A\in{\mathcal{E}}
and a pseudonatural transformation
{\mathcal{E}}[J{-},A]\Rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}[T{-},A]
. This shows that the pseudonaturality structure of pseudoalgebras is coherent in an appropriate two-dimensional sense, and explains why the composition of lax morphisms of pseudoalgebras is reminiscent of the composition of lax natural transformations. More abstractly, it lays the groundwork for presenting the bicategories of pseudoalgebras as certain three-dimensional limits. Though we do not pursue this here, it would be a natural starting point for a formal theory of relative pseudomonads (cf.
[
Lac00
,
AM24
]
).

The following extends
[
FGHW18
, Proposition 4.7(iii)]
from free pseudoalgebras to arbitrary pseudoalgebras, and confirms that the unitor
\eta
(
Definition

2.7
) is indeed a modification.

.

Let

Next, we study the interaction between the pseudonatural transformations induced by each pseudoalgebra. It will be useful to introduce a preliminary definition.

Definition 3.23
.

{\mathcal{B}}

•

in

and a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gf}

•

are 2-cells

in

•

The composite
(X,x)\xrightarrow{(f,\phi)}(Y,y)\xrightarrow{(g,\gamma)}(Z,z)
is defined to be
gf
equipped with the following 2-cell.

(11)

z\circ F(gf)\xrightarrow{\cong}(z\circ Fg)\circ Ff\xrightarrow{\gamma\circ Ff}G(g\circ y)\circ Ff\xrightarrow{\cong}Gg\circ(y\circ Ff)\xrightarrow{Gg\circ\phi}Gg\circ(Gf\circ x)\xrightarrow{\cong}G(gf)\circ x

The identity on
(X,x)
is defined to be
1_{X}
equipped with the 2-cell
x\circ F(1_{X})\xrightarrow{\cong}G(1_{X})\circ x
.

•

The associators and unitors are inherited from
{\mathcal{A}}
.

By requiring each
\phi
in the definition of the morphisms to be invertible, we obtain the
pseudo-inserter

\mathrm{Ins}_{p}(F,G)
. By requiring it to be the identity, we obtain the
strict-inserter

\mathrm{Ins}_{s}(F,G)
. By reversing the direction of
\phi
, we obtain the
colax-inserter

\mathrm{Ins}_{c}(F,G)
.

For each
w\in\{s,p,l,c\}
we write
\pi
for each of the evident strict pseudofunctor
\mathrm{Ins}_{w}(F,G)\to{\mathcal{A}}
.

Remark 3.24
.

The lax-inserter of two pseudofunctors is a three-dimensional notion of limit living in the tricategory of bicategories, which generalises a two-dimensional notion of limit known as an
inserter

[
Lac10
, §6.5]
. Note in particular that the pseudo-inserter of two pseudofunctors is distinct from the similarly-named
iso-inserter
(as studied in the context of pseudofunctors in
[
Gur13
, Definition 2.14, Lemma 2.16 & Remark 2.16]
, for instance).

The
nerve
of a pseudofunctor
F\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
is the pseudofunctor
N_{F}\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathbf{Bicat}({\mathcal{A}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Cat})
defined by
N_{F}\mathrel{:=}{\mathcal{B}}[F{-}_{2},{-}_{1}]
. In particular, for a
J
-relative pseudomonad
T
we have pseudofunctors
N_{J},N_{T}\colon{\mathcal{E}}\to\mathbf{Bicat}({\mathcal{A}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Cat})
. The following result shows that the bicategory structure of
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
(i.e. the composition, identities, and structural transformations) coincides with that of
\mathrm{Ins}_{l}(N_{J},N_{T})
. This demonstrates, for instance, that the composition of lax morphisms may be seen as precisely the composition of lax natural transformations.

Proposition 3.25
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor and let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad. For each
w\in\{s,p,l,c\}
, there is a canonical locally faithful strict pseudofunctor
K\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}_{w}(T)\to\mathrm{Ins}_{w}(N_{J},N_{T})
rendering the following diagram commutative.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{PsAlg}_{w}(T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Ins}_{w}(N_{J},N_{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Proof.

We show the result for
w=l
; the other cases are straightforward adjustments.
For the action on objects, we use
Item

3.22.(1)
and define
K\big((A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})\big)\mathrel{:=}(A,{({-})}^{a})
.
For the action on 1-cells, consider a lax morphism

(h,\overline{h})\colon(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})\to(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
.
We shall show that the 2-cells
\overline{h}_{f}
form a modification filling the square on the left below in
\mathbf{Bicat}({\mathcal{A}}{}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Cat})
, so that we may define
K\big((h,\overline{h})\big)\mathrel{:=}(h,\overline{h})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[J{-},A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[J{-},B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[T{-},A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[T{-},B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}[J{-},h]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}[T{-},h]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}}

The modification axiom states that, for each
f\colon Y\to X
, the following 2-cells are equal.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JY,A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TX,A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JY,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TX,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TY,B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[Jf,A]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(-)^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,h]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JY,h]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TX,h]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{h}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[Jf,B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(-)^{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(-)^{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[Tf,B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(-)^{b}_{f}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JY,A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TX,A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TY,A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JY,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TX,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TY,B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[Jf,A]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(-)^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JY,h]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}[Tf,A]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TX,h]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}[TY,h]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(-)^{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[Tf,B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{a}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Using the definition of the pseudonatural transformation
{({-})}^{a}
in (
9
), for each 1-cell
k\colon JX\to A
, this equation becomes the commutativity of the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hkJf)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big((hk)^{b}i_{X}Jf\big)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(kJf)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hk^{a}i_{X}Jf)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hk)^{b}(i_{X}Jf)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h\big(k^{a}i_{X}Jf\big)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hk^{a}(i_{X}Jf)^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\tilde{b}_{hk}Jf)^{b}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\overline{h}_{kJf}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(h\tilde{a}_{k}Jf)^{b}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\overline{h}_{k}i_{X}Jf)^{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{b}_{hk,i_{X}Jf}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{nat.\ of }\overline{h}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{h(\tilde{a}_{k}Jf)^{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{h}_{k^{a}i_{X}Jf}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{h}_{k}Tf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h\hat{a}_{k,i_{X}Jf}}}
h
unit.
h
assoc.

Turning to the transformations, if
\alpha\colon(h,\overline{h})\Rightarrow(h^{\prime},\overline{h^{\prime}})
is an algebra transformation, then
\alpha
is a 2-cell in
\mathrm{Ins}_{w}(N_{J},N_{T})
: indeed, the transformation axiom in
Definition

3.7
is exactly the condition (
10
). So we may define
K(\alpha)\mathrel{:=}\alpha
. This is clearly functorial with respect to vertical composition, and locally faithful. Strict functoriality of
K
follows by unwinding the definitions of composition and identities in both bicategories.
∎

4.
Doctrinal adjunction and transport of structure

In this section, we explore how pseudoalgebras interact with adjunctions. It was observed by
[
Kel74
]
that, given an adjunction between monoidal categories, colax monoidal structures on the left adjoint are in bijection with lax monoidal structures on the right adjoint. This situation in fact holds, much more generally, for adjunctions between the pseudoalgebras for a 2-monad
[
Kel74
]
. This phenomenon was dubbed
doctrinal adjunction
(a
doctrine
being another term for a 2-monad), and proves to be a remarkably useful tool. We show in this section that doctrinal adjunction holds also for pseudoalgebras for relative pseudomonads; this generalises both
[
Kel74
]
’s original setting, and doctrinal adjunction for pseudomonads as studied by
[
Nun17
, Theorem 1.4.14]
and
[
Wal19
, Corollary 78]
. Doctrinal adjunction will be useful in
Section

5
when we study lax-idempotence for pseudoalgebras, and subsequently to characterise the pseudoalgebras for the presheaf construction in
Section

7
. Below,
Theorem

4.1
is the analogue of
[
Kel74
, Theorem 1.2]
, while
Proposition

4.2
is the analogue of
[
Kel74
, Theorems 1.4 & 1.5]
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor, let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad, and let
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
and
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
be
T
-pseudoalgebras. For any adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

between their carriers in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the following are in bijection:

(4.1.(1))

colax morphism structures on
\ell
;

(4.1.(2))

lax morphism structures on
r
.

Proof.

We write
\nu\colon 1_{A}\Rightarrow r\ell
and
\varepsilon\colon\ell r\Rightarrow 1_{B}
for the unit and counit of the adjunction respectively. Suppose that
r
has a lax morphism structure. We define a putative colax morphism structure on
\ell
by setting
\overline{\ell}_{f}
to be the mate of
\overline{r}_{f}
for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
.

(12)

\overline{\ell}_{f}\mathrel{:=}\Big(\ell f^{a}\xrightarrow{\ell(\nu f)^{a}}\ell(r\ell f)^{a}\xrightarrow{\ell\overline{r}_{\ell f}}\ell r(\ell f)^{b}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon(\ell f)^{b}}(\ell f)^{b}\Big)

For the associativity law, we need to fill the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell\big(g^{a}f)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ell g^{a}f)^{b}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ell g)^{a}f^{*}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\hat{a}_{g,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\ell}_{g}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\ell}_{g}f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{b}_{\ell g,f}}

We do this as follows; the unlabelled cells use the functoriality of vertical composition and (

) is the right triangle law for the adjunction. Note that the boundary of the following diagram is by definition the boundary of the preceding diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell\big(g^{a}f)^{a}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell(r\ell g)^{a}f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell\big(r(\ell g)^{b}f\big)^{a}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell r\big(\ell r(\ell g)^{b}f\big)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\ell r(\ell g)^{b}f\big)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell\big(r(\ell g)^{b}f\big)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell r(\ell g)^{a}f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell r\big((\ell g)^{b}f\big)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ell g)^{a}f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big((\ell g)^{b}f\big)^{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell(\nu g^{a}f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\hat{a}_{g,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell((\nu g)^{a}f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\overline{r}_{\ell g^{a}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell(r\ell(\nu g)^{a}f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon(\ell g^{a}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell r(\ell(\nu g)^{a}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ell(\nu g)^{a}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\hat{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell(\nu g)^{a}f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell(\nu(r\ell g)^{a}f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\hat{a}_{r\ell g,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell(\overline{r}_{\ell g}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\overline{r}_{\ell(r\ell g)^{a}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell(r\ell\overline{r}_{\ell g}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon(\ell(r\ell g)^{a}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell r(\ell\overline{r}_{\ell g}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ell\overline{r}_{\ell g}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell r_{\ell g}f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell(\nu r(\ell g)^{b}f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\overline{r}_{\ell r(\ell g)^{b}f}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon(\ell r(\ell g)^{b}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell r(\varepsilon(\ell g)^{b}f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varepsilon(\ell g)^{b}f)^{b}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{b}_{\ell g,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\overline{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\overline{r}}

assoc.
(
\scriptstyle*
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\overline{r}}

The proof of the unit law is similar, as shown below, where (

) is the left triangle law.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell f}}

)
r

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\ell}_{f}i}

Thus
(\ell,\overline{\ell})
is a colax morphism.
A dual argument shows that every colax morphism structure on

induces a lax morphism structure on

, by defining
\overline{r}_{g}\colon(rg)^{a}\to rg^{b}
to be the mate of
\overline{l}_{f}
for each 1-cell

.

(13)

\overline{r}_{g}\mathrel{:=}\Big((rg)^{a}\xrightarrow{\nu(rg)^{a}}r\ell(rg)^{a}\xrightarrow{r\overline{\ell}_{rg}}r(\ell rg)^{b}\xrightarrow{r(\varepsilon g)^{b}}rg^{b}\Big)

Finally, these assignments form a bijection by the usual calculus of mates.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

Under the same assumptions as
Theorem

4.1
:

(4.2.(1))

the adjunction
\ell\dashv r
lifts to
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
if and only if
\overline{\ell}
is invertible, i.e.
(\ell,\overline{\ell})
is a pseudomorphism from
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
to
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
;

(4.2.(2))

the adjunction
\ell\dashv r
lifts to
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{c}(T)
if and only if
\overline{r}
is invertible, i.e.
(r,\overline{r})
is a pseudomorphism.

Moreover, in each case the inverse is constructed by taking mates.

Proof.

We prove (1); the argument for (2) is dual. Suppose
(\ell,\overline{\ell})
is a pseudomorphism; we need to show that the unit
\nu\colon 1_{A}\Rightarrow r\ell
and counit
\varepsilon\colon\ell r\Rightarrow 1_{B}
each satisfy the transformation axiom (
3.7
), so that
(\ell,\overline{\ell}^{-1})\colon(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})\rightleftarrows(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu(r,\overline{r})
is an adjunction in
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
. This is shown by the two diagrams below, in which (
*
) indicates the left triangle law in each case.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(r\ell f)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\ell f^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\ell(r\ell f)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(\ell f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(\ell r\ell f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(\ell f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\ell f^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\nu f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu f^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu(r\ell f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\ell(\nu f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\overline{\ell}_{f})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\overline{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\overline{\ell}_{r\ell f})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\ell\nu f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\varepsilon\ell f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\ell_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu f^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(*)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{r}_{\ell f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ell rg)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell(rg)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell r\ell(rg)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell(rg)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell r(\ell rg)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ell rg)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell rg^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varepsilon g)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\ell}_{rg}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\nu(rg)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon\ell(rg)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell r(\overline{\ell}_{rg})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\ell}_{rg})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon(\ell rg)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell r(\varepsilon g)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varepsilon g)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon g^{b}}
(
\scriptstyle*
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\overline{r}_{g}}

For the converse, suppose that
\ell
acquires a lax algebra morphism structure
(\ell,\overline{\ell})
such that
(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon(\ell,\overline{\ell})\dashv(r,\overline{r})
is an adjunction in
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
. Then the 2-cells
\nu
and
\varepsilon
must lift to
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
, so the cells labelled (
*
), and hence both diagrams below, must commute.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell f^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell(r\ell f)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell r(\ell f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ell f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell f^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell r\ell f^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell f^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell(\nu f)^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\overline{r}_{\ell f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon(\ell f)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell r\overline{\ell}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\ell}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\nu f^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon\ell f^{a}}
(
\scriptstyle*
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ell f)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ell r\ell g)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\ell g)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell f^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell(r\ell g)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell rg^{b}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\overline{l}}

)

Now, the bottom composite on the left and the top composite on the right are, by the left triangle law, both equal to the identity. So
\overline{\ell}_{f}
is invertible, with inverse the mate of
\overline{r}_{f}
defined in (
