).
∎

Remark 4.3
.

In the preceding theorem, the induced colax morphism structure on
\overline{l}_{f}
is invertible if and only if the composite (
12
) is invertible. In the non-relative setting, Nunes calls this the
Beck–Chevalley condition
on the lax morphism
r

[
Nun17
, Definition 1.4.13]
.

A variant of doctrinal adjunction concerns when an object of
{\mathcal{E}}
is reflective (or coreflective) in a pseudoalgebra, giving sufficient conditions for pseudoalgebra structure to be inherited via the reflection. In particular, these conditions hold when the reflection is an equivalence. In this way, we obtain a
transport of structure
theorem for pseudoalgebras, which shows that pseudoalgebra structure may be transferred across an equivalence in
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Corollary 4.4

(Transport of structure)
.

Let
(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon\ell\colon A\leftrightarrows B\colon r
be a coreflective adjunction in
{\mathcal{E}}
(so that
\nu
is invertible) and let
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
be a
T
-pseudoalgebra. If the 2-cell

(14)

r(\ell rf)^{b}\xrightarrow{r(\varepsilon f)^{b}}rf^{b}

is invertible for every
f\colon JX\to B
, then:

(4.4.(1))

A
acquires a
T
-pseudoalgebra structure;

(4.4.(2))

r
acquires the structure of an algebra pseudomorphism and
\ell
acquires the structure of a colax algebra morphism;

(4.4.(3))

the unit
\nu
and counit
\varepsilon
are algebra transformations.

Hence, the adjunction
(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon\ell\dashv r
lifts to an adjunction in
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{c}(T)
. In particular, if
(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon\ell\dashv r
is an adjoint equivalence then it lifts to
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
. Conversely, if

(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon\ell\dashv r

lifts to an adjunction in
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{c}(T)
then the 2-cell (
14
)
is invertible for every
f\colon JX\to B
in
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

We start with the forward direction.
For the algebra structure on
A
, we define an extension operator as follows:

(15)

(-)^{a}_{X}\mathrel{:=}{\mathcal{E}}[JX,A]\xrightarrow{\ell\circ-}{\mathcal{E}}[JX,B]\xrightarrow{(-)^{b}_{X}}{\mathcal{E}}[TX,B]\xrightarrow{r\circ-}{\mathcal{E}}[TX,A]

We define
\tilde{a}
by
\tilde{a}_{f}\mathrel{:=}\big(f\xrightarrow{\nu f}r\ell f\xrightarrow{f\tilde{b}_{\ell f}}r(\ell f)^{b}i_{X}=f^{a}i_{X}\big)
for each
f\colon JX\to A
; this is invertible since
\nu
is. For
\hat{a}
, we note that
(g^{a}f)^{a}=r\big(\ell r(\ell g)^{b}f\big)^{b}
, so that we may define
\hat{a}
as follows:

\hat{a}_{g,f}\mathrel{:=}\Big((g^{a}f)^{a}=r\big(\ell r(\ell g)^{b}f\big)^{b}\xrightarrow{r(\varepsilon(\ell g)^{b}f)^{b}}r\big((\ell g)^{b}f\big)^{b}\xrightarrow{r\hat{b}_{\ell g,f}}r(\ell g)^{b}f^{*}=g^{a}f^{*}\Big)

The invertibility condition (
14
) ensures this is invertible. Next, we need to verify the associativity and unit laws in
Definition

3.1
. For the associativity law, observe first that the following equations hold:

\big((h^{a}g)^{a}f\big)^{a}=r\big[\ell r\big(\ell r(\ell h)^{b}g\big)^{b}f\big]^{b}\qquad\qquad(h^{a}g^{*}f)^{a}=r\big(\ell r(\ell h)^{b}q^{*}f\big)^{b}

The associativity law then unwinds to the next diagram, in which the unlabelled shapes are instances of the functoriality of composition.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\big[\ell r\big(\ell r(\ell h)^{b}g\big)^{b}f\big]^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\big[\big(\ell r(\ell h)^{b}g\big)^{b}f\big]^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\big(\ell r(\ell h)^{b}g\big)^{b}f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\big[\ell r\big((\ell h)^{b}g\big)^{b}f\big]^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\big[\ell r(\ell h)^{b}g^{*}f\big]^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\big[\big((\ell h)^{b}g\big)^{b}f\big]^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\big[(\ell h)^{b}g^{*}f\big]^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\big((\ell h)^{b}g\big)^{b}f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(\ell h)^{b}\big(g^{*}f\big)^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(\ell h)^{b}g^{*}f^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r[\varepsilon\big(\ell r(\ell h)^{b}g)^{b}f]^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r[\ell r(\varepsilon(\ell h)^{b}g)^{b}f]^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\hat{b}_{\ell r(\ell h)^{b}g,f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\big[\big(\varepsilon(\ell h)^{b}g\big)^{b}f\big]^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\big(\varepsilon(\ell h)^{b}g\big)^{b}f^{*}}

For the unit law we make use of the left triangle law for the adjunction

So

). To this end, define

as follows, which is invertible since (

\overline{r}_{f}\mathrel{:=}\Big((rf)^{a}=r(\ell rf)^{b}\xrightarrow{r(\varepsilon f)^{b}}rf^{b}\Big)

For the associativity law for

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rf}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{rf^{a}i_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu rf}

-algebra pseudomorphism
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})\to(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
.

The rest of the claim now follows. Indeed, by
Theorem

4.1
we see that
\ell
acquires a colax morphism structure as in (
12
). Next, applying

4.2
, the adjunction
(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon\ell\dashv r
lifts to
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{c}(T)
. In particular, if we start with an adjoint equivalence then
\nu
,
\varepsilon
and
\overline{r}_{f}
are all invertible, so
\ell
becomes a pseudomorphism and the adjunction lifts to
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
. Moreover, since the forgetful pseudofunctor
U_{T}
is locally conservative (
Proposition

3.13
), this too is an adjoint equivalence.

Turning now to the converse, suppose that the adjunction

(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon\ell\dashv r

lifts to an adjunction

(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon(\ell,\overline{\ell})\dashv(r,\overline{r})

in
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{c}(T)
. We want to show that
r(\varepsilon f)^{b}
is invertible for every

f\colon JX\to B
. Observe first that
r(\varepsilon f)^{b}i_{X}
is invertible. This follows from the commutativity of the following diagram, in which every vertical arrow is, by assumption, an isomorphism, and the unlabelled region is the right triangle law.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{rf}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r\ell rf}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(\ell rf)^{b}i_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{rf^{b}i_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu rf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\tilde{b}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\varepsilon f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\ell r\tilde{b}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\varepsilon f)^{b}i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\tilde{b}}

From

4.2
, since the adjunction

, the 2-cell
\overline{r}

r(\ell rf)^{b}\xrightarrow{r(\varepsilon f)^{b}}rf^{b}\xrightarrow{\overline{r}_{f}}(rf)^{a}

This follows from the commutativity of the following diagram, since every arrow in the clockwise path is an isomorphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(\ell rf)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{r(\ell rf)^{b}(i_{X})^{*}}}

Remark 4.5
.

The reader may observe that the proof of
Corollary

4.4
actually show how to construct a lax algebra structure and lax algebra morphism from an adjunction. However, since (as discussed in
Remark

3.4
) in this paper we are concerned with pseudoalgebras, we have imposed additional assumptions to ensure invertibility of the algebra structure.

For completeness, we also include the dual result, which is the analogue of
[
Kel74
, Theorem 3.1]
.

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon\ell\colon A\rightleftarrows B\colon r
be a reflective adjunction in
{\mathcal{E}}
(so that
\varepsilon
is invertible) and let
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
be a
T
-pseudoalgebra. If the 2-cell

(16)

\ell f^{a}\xrightarrow{\ell(\nu f)^{a}}\ell(r\ell f)^{a}

is invertible for every
f\colon JX\to A
, then:

(4.6.(1))

B
acquires a
T
-pseudoalgebra structure;

(4.6.(2))

\ell
acquires the structure of an algebra pseudomorphism and
r
acquires the structure of a lax algebra morphism;

(4.6.(3))

the unit
\nu
and counit
\varepsilon
are algebra transformations.

Hence, the adjunction
(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon\ell\dashv r
lifts to an adjunction in
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
. Conversely, if
(\nu,\varepsilon)\colon\ell\dashv r
lifts to an adjunction in
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
then the 2-cell (
16
)
is invertible for every
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

Recall that an adjunction
\ell\colon A\rightleftarrows B\colon r
in
{\mathcal{E}}
with unit

\nu\colon 1_{A}\to r\ell

and counit

\varepsilon\colon\ell r\to 1_{B}

is equivalently an adjunction in
{\mathcal{E}}{}^{\mathrm{co}}
with left adjoint
r
, right adjoint
\ell
, unit
\varepsilon
, and counit
\nu
.
In this light, the conditions given are precisely the assumptions of
Corollary

4.4
with respect to the adjunction

r\dashv\ell

in
{\mathcal{E}}{}^{\mathrm{co}}
. The claim then follows once one observes that a pseudoalgebra/pseudomorphism in
{\mathcal{E}}{}^{\mathrm{co}}
is equivalently a pseudoalgebra/pseudomorphism in
{\mathcal{E}}
, while a colax algebra morphism in
{\mathcal{E}}{}^{\mathrm{co}}
is equivalently a lax algebra morphism in
{\mathcal{E}}
.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

U_{T}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)\to{\mathcal{E}}
is an equifibration in the sense of
[
Cam20
, Definition 4.1]
.

Proof.

The adjoint equivalence lifting property follows from
Corollary

4.4
, while the local isofibration property follows from
Proposition

3.13
.
∎

5.
Lax-idempotent pseudoalgebras

As hinted at in
Examples

3.6
and
3.8
, the presheaf construction is a particularly well-behaved relative pseudomonad. As shown in
[
FGHW18
, §5]
, it is an example of a
lax-idempotent relative pseudomonad
. In the non-relative setting, the lax-idempotent pseudomonads are, intuitively, those that correspond to cocompletions under a class of colimits
[
Koc95
,
Zöb76
,
PCW00
]
; the notion of lax-idempotence for relative pseudomonads was introduced to capture analogous behaviour in the relative setting. To characterise the pseudoalgebras for the presheaf construction (as well as a family of related constructions exhibiting free cocompletions under other classes of colimits), it will be useful to develop the theory of lax-idempotence for relative pseudomonads further. In particular, we will observe that there is an unexpected bifurcation in the move from pseudomonads to relative pseudomonads that renders the notion of lax-idempotence introduced by
[
FGHW18
]
too weak to establish several theorems of interest. The following notion of lax-idempotence for pseudoalgebras is intended to rectify this problem, as we will explain shortly.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor and let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad. A
T
-pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is
lax-idempotent
6
6

6

As a compound adjective, it is appropriate always to hyphenate
lax-idempotent
, and hence also
lax-idempotence
. In particular,
lax
is not acting as an adverb in this context: lax-idempotence should be viewed as a property reminiscent of, but not a particular case of, idempotence.
if
\tilde{a}
exhibits the unit of an adjunction

(17)

{\mathcal{E}}[JX,A]\colon{({-})}^{a}\dashv{({-})}\circ i_{X}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{\mathcal{E}}[TX,A]

for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
.

Let us immediately note that the definition of lax-idempotence for pseudoalgebras may be rephrased in terms of left extensions, as this perspective will be helpful later.

Lemma 5.2
.

A
T
-pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is lax-idempotent if and only if
\tilde{a}_{f}\colon f\Rightarrow f^{a}i_{X}
exhibits
f^{a}
as the left extension of
f
along
i_{X}
for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

By definition,
{\mathcal{E}}
admits left extensions along
i_{X}
if and only if the precomposition functor
{({-})}\circ i_{X}
admits a left adjoint
[
Lac10
, §2.2]
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

The pseudoalgebra is
pseudo-idempotent
if (
17
) is furthermore an adjoint equivalence. Dually, a
T
-pseudoalgebra is
colax-idempotent
if
\tilde{a}^{-1}
exhibits the counit of an adjunction

{\mathcal{E}}[TX,A]\colon{({-})}\circ i_{X}\dashv{({-})}^{a}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{\mathcal{E}}[JX,A]

for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
. Just as lax-idempotence is intended to capture colimit-like properties, colax-idempotence is intended to limit-like properties, while pseudo-idempotence captures properties that are both limit- and colimit-like, such as free (co)completions under absolute (co)limits. While we will focus on lax-idempotence in what follows, the results of this section carry through with appropriate modifications for pseudo- and colax-idempotent pseudoalgebras.

The notion of
lax-idempotent relative pseudomonad
was introduced in
[
FGHW18
, §5]
. We shall take the following characterisation as the definition.
7
7

7

In particular, this characterisation justifies the simpler definition of lax-idempotence utilised in
[
Sla23
, Definition 5.1]
(cf.
[
FGHW18
, (5.1)]
).

Proposition 5.4
.

A relative pseudomonad is lax-idempotent in the sense of
[
FGHW18
, Definition 5.1]
if and only if every free pseudoalgebra is lax-idempotent, i.e.
\eta
exhibits the unit of an adjunction

{\mathcal{E}}[JX,TY]\colon{({-})}^{*}_{X,Y}\dashv{({-})}\circ i_{X}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{\mathcal{E}}[TX,TY]

for each
X,Y\in{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

[
FGHW18
, Definition 5.1]
asks for the stated condition subject to two compatibility conditions. However, the two compatibility conditions are in fact redundant, following from
[
FGHW18
, Lemma 3.2(i & iii)]
.
∎

Consequently, the free pseudoalgebras for a relative pseudomonad that is lax-idempotent in the sense of
[
FGHW18
]
are themselves lax-idempotent. However, crucially, this is
not
generally true for arbitary pseudoalgebras: that is, there exist lax-idempotent relative pseudomonads whose pseudoalgebras are not lax-idempotent. The following provides a particularly simple counterexample in one dimension.

Example 5.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the free category on the following graph,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

subject to
fi=f^{\prime}i
. We can view
J
and
T
as functors
1\to{\mathcal{E}}
from the terminal category.
T
may then be equipped with a relative monad structure
T
: the unit is
i
, and the extension operator sends the morphism
i
to the identity on
T
: this is trivially a bijection because
T
has no nontrivial endomorphisms. Thus
T
is lax-idempotent (in fact, it is strictly idempotent). However,
E
has two distinct algebra structures: one sends the unique map
J\to E
to
f
, and the other sends the unique map to
f^{\prime}
. Thus
E
admits an algebra structure that is not lax-idempotent.

The existence of
Example

5.5
may be surprising, because in the
non-relative
setting, no counterexamples exist; this explains why the definition of
[
FGHW18
]
seems reasonable when one does not consider non-free pseudoalgebras.

Proposition 5.6
.

A (non-relative) pseudomonad
T
is lax-idempotent if and only if every
T
-pseudoalgebra is lax-idempotent.

Proof.

It follows immediately from
Proposition

5.4
that if every
T
-pseudoalgebra is lax-idempotent, then so is
T
. For the converse, note that a lax-idempotent pseudomonad is equivalently a left extension pseudomonad
[
MW12
,
FGHW18
]
. Every pseudoalgebra for such is equivalently a left extension pseudoalgebra by
[
MW12
, §5]
, hence lax-idempotent by
Lemma

5.2
.
∎

The above considerations suggest that a more appropriate definition of lax-idempotence for relative pseudomonads would be to ask that
every
pseudoalgebra is lax-idempotent. We will see in the remainder of this section that this stronger notion satisfies various expected properties known to hold for lax-idempotent (non-relative) pseudomonads. However, to avoid confusion with
[
FGHW18
]
, we will continue to use
lax-idempotent relative pseudomonad
in this paper to refer to the weaker notion appearing in
Proposition

5.4
.

The following characterisation of lax-idempotent pseudoalgebras in terms of lax morphisms, together with
Corollary

5.8
, motivates the terminology
lax-idempotent
(in the terminology of
[
KL97
]
, this is a strengthening of
property (AEL)
).

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor and let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad. A
T
-pseudoalgebra
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
is lax-idempotent if and only if, for every
T
-pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
, every 1-cell
f\colon A\to B
in
{\mathcal{E}}
admits a unique lax morphism structure
\overline{f}
. In this case,

\overline{f}_{g}\colon(fg)^{b}\Rightarrow fg^{a}

is the unique 2-cell corresponding to

f\tilde{a}_{g}\colon fg\Rightarrow fg^{a}i_{X}

under the adjunction (
17
).

Proof.

(
\implies
)
Suppose
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
is a lax-idempotent
T
-pseudoalgebra, so that we have adjunctions
(\tilde{b},\beta)\colon(-)^{b}\dashv(-)\circ i_{X}
for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
. Let
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
be a
T
-pseudoalgebra and let
f\colon A\to B
be a 1-cell in
{\mathcal{E}}
.
If
f
were to have lax morphism structure then each component
\overline{f}_{g}\colon(fg)^{b}\Rightarrow fg^{a}
would correspond under the adjunction
(
17
) to the following composite:

(18)

fg\xrightarrow{\tilde{b}_{fg}}(fg)^{b}i_{X}\xrightarrow{\overline{f}_{g}i_{X}}fg^{a}i_{X}

By the unit law for lax morphisms (
3.5.(4)
), this composite is equal to
f\tilde{a}_{g}\colon fg\Rightarrow fg^{a}i_{X}
. Hence supposing lax morphism structure exists, it is necessarily unique.

It remains to show that (
18
) indeed gives
f
the structure of a lax morphism. The unit law (
3.5.(4)
) holds by definition; it remains to show the associativity law (
3.5.(3)
).
We do so by taking transposes, and filling in the resulting diagram as shown below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hg)^{b}f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((hg)^{b}f)^{b}i_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((hg)^{b}f)^{b}i_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hg^{a}f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hg)^{b}f^{*}i_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(hg^{a}f)^{b}i_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h(g^{a}f)^{a}i_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{hg^{a}f^{*}i_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}_{(hg)^{b}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}_{(hg)^{b}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{g}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hg)^{b}\eta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{b}_{hg,f}i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{h}_{g}f)^{b}i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}_{hg^{a}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\tilde{a}_{g^{a}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces hg^{a}\eta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{g}f^{*}i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}_{g^{a}f}i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\hat{a}_{g,f}i_{X}}
B
unit.
′
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\tilde{b}}
A
unit.
′
h
unit.

Thus, defining
\overline{f}_{g}
to be the transpose of
f\tilde{a}_{g}
gives
f
the structure of a lax morphism.

(
\impliedby
) Let
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
be a
T
-pseudoalgebra such that, for every
T
-pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
and 1-cell
f\colon A\to B
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, there is a unique choice of 2-cells
\overline{f}
equipping
f
with the structure of a lax morphism
(f,\overline{f})
. Fix
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
. We must find a natural transformation

\beta\colon({-}\circ i_{X})^{b}\Rightarrow 1
forming the counit of an adjunction

(\tilde{b},\beta)\colon(-)^{b}\dashv(-)\circ i_{X}
. Taking
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
to be the free
T
-pseudoalgebra on
X
(
Proposition

3.19
), we have that, for each
f\colon TX\to B
, there exists a unique lax morphism structure on
f
with components

\overline{f}_{g}\colon(fg)^{b}\Rightarrow fg^{*}
.
So we may define the components of
\beta
to be as follows:

\beta_{f}\mathrel{:=}\Big((fi_{X})^{b}\xrightarrow{\overline{f}_{i}}fi_{X}^{*}\xrightarrow{f\theta_{X}}f\Big)

We now need to check the triangle laws, which are the following two diagrams.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{fi_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(fi_{X})^{b}i_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(i_{X})^{*}i_{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{fi_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}_{fi_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\eta_{i_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{i_{X}}i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\theta_{X}i_{X}}
f
unit.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(g^{b}i_{X})^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{b}(i_{X})^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g^{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{b}_{g})^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{g^{b}}=\hat{b}_{g,i_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{b}\theta_{X}}
B
unit.

The unlabelled triangle in the left-hand diagram is
[
FGHW18
, Lemma 3.2(iii)]
.
It remains to show that the family
\beta
is natural. This follows from commutativity of the following diagram, for each
f,f^{\prime}\colon TX\to B
and
\rho\colon f\Rightarrow f^{\prime}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(fi_{X})^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(i_{X})^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{\prime}i_{X})^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime}(i_{X})^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{i_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\rho i_{X})^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\theta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(i_{X})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f^{\prime}}_{i_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}\theta_{X}}
ρ
trans.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{f^{\prime}}}

Hence
\beta
is indeed the counit of an adjunction

(\tilde{b},\beta)\colon(-)^{b}\dashv(-)\circ i_{X}

witnessing
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
as lax-idempotent.
∎

As in the non-relative setting (see
[
KL97
, §6]
), the observation that 1-cells have unique lax morphism structure has a number of useful further consequences. In the terminology of
[
KL97
]
, the next lemma proves an analogue of
property (2L)
: every 2-cell between lax morphisms with lax-idempotent codomain is a transformation.

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor,
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad,
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
be an
T
-pseudoalgebra, and
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
be a lax-idempotent
T
-pseudoalgebra. The forgetful functor

U_{T}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T){\big[(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a}),(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})\big]}\to{\mathcal{E}}[A,B]

given by the action of the strict pseudofunctor
U_{T}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)\to{\mathcal{E}}
is fully faithful.

Proof.

Faithfulness follows from
Proposition

3.13
.
For fullness, let
\phi\colon f\Rightarrow f^{\prime}
be any 2-cell in
{\mathcal{E}}
. By
Theorem

5.7
,
f
and
f^{\prime}
have unique lax algebra structures
\overline{f}
and
\overline{f^{\prime}}
, respectively. To show that
\phi
is a transformation we need to show the square below commutes for all
g\colon JX\to A
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(fg)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{\prime}g)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{fg^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime}g^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi g)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f^{\prime}}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi g^{a}}

By
Theorem

5.7
,
(fg)^{b}\xrightarrow{\overline{f}_{g}}fg^{a}

is equal to the composite

(19)

(fg)^{b}\xrightarrow{(f\tilde{a}_{g})^{b}}(fg^{a}i_{X})^{b}\xrightarrow{\beta_{fg^{a}}}fg^{a}

(and similarly for
f^{\prime}
), where
\beta
is the counit of the adjunction (
17
).
Hence the required naturality square decomposes as follows, and so every 2-cell is a transformation.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(fg)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{\prime}g)^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(fg^{a}i_{X})^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{\prime}g^{a}i_{X})^{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{fg^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{\prime}g^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi g)^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\tilde{a}_{g})^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{\prime}\tilde{a}_{g})^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi g^{a}i_{X})^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{fg^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{f^{\prime}g^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi g^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\beta}

∎

One useful consequence of the preceding results is the observation that left adjoint 1-cells between lax-idempotent algebras are always pseudomorphisms. This agrees with our intuition that lax-idempotence corresponds to colimit structure, as left adjoints always preserve colimits. The following was proven in the strict setting in
[
Koc95
, Proposition 2.5]
and for pseudomonads in
[
Mar97
, Proposition 5.1]
(both under the stronger assumption that the pseudomonad itself is lax-idempotent). As observed in
[
Kel74
]
, it is a consequence of doctrinal adjunction.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor, let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad, and let
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
and
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
be lax-idempotent
T
-pseudoalgebras. Every left adjoint 1-cell
A\to B
in
{\mathcal{E}}
is a pseudomorphism of
T
-pseudoalgebras.

Proof.

Let
\ell\colon A\rightleftarrows B\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mur
be an adjunction in
{\mathcal{E}}
. Since
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
and
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
are lax-idempotent,
\ell
and
r
acquire unique lax morphism structures by
Theorem

5.7
, for which the unit and counit 2-cells are transformations by
Corollary

5.8
, so that the adjunction lifts to an adjunction in
\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)
. Consequently, by
Proposition

4.2
,
\ell
is a pseudomorphism.
∎

This in turn implies that lax-idempotent pseudoalgebra structure is essentially unique.

Corollary 5.10
.

Any two lax-idempotent pseudoalgebra structures on the same object are equivalent.

Proof.

By
Propositions

5.9
and
5.8
, the trivial adjunction
1_{A}\dashv 1_{A}
in
{\mathcal{E}}
extends to an adjoint equivalence between any pair of pseudoalgebras with the same carrier in
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

Together with
Theorem

5.7
, this shows that lax-idempotent pseudoalgebra structure is
property-like
in the sense of
[
KL97
, §4]
. However, we do not know whether they are furthermore
fully property-like
in the sense of
[
KL97
, §5]
, which would require every 2-cell between colax morphisms to have the structure of a transformation (see
Section

5.2
for further discussion).

Finally, we observe that lax-idempotent structure transports across reflections.

Proposition 5.12
.

Under the assumptions of
Corollary

4.6
, if the
T
-pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is lax-idempotent, then so is the induced
T
-pseudoalgebra structure on
B
.

Proof.

For each object
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
, we have adjunctions as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,B]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TX,A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[TX,B]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\circ{({-})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\circ{({-})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}^{a}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({-})}\circ i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\circ{({-})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\circ{({-})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Consequently, by
[
ADL24
, Lemma 2.7]
, we have
\ell\circ(r{({-})})^{a}_{X}\dashv\ell r{({-})}\circ i_{X}\cong{({-})}\circ i_{X}
, where the left adjoint is precisely the extension operator
{({-})}^{b}_{X}
for the induced pseudoalgebra structure on
B
(cf. (
15
)), and it is straightforward to verify the unit is precisely
\tilde{b}
.
∎

5.1.
Left extension pseudoalgebras

In
[
FGHW18
, §5]
, building upon the work of
[
MW12
]
, the authors showed that the definition of a relative pseudomonad could be simplified under the assumption of lax-idempotence, by taking advantage of the universal properties of left extensions. We shall show the same is true for pseudoalgebras, extending
[
MW12
, §3 & §5]
. The following notion was considered in
[
Lew20
, Definition 4.36]
by the name of
relative
T
-complete object
.

Definition 5.13
.

Let
T
be a relative pseudomonad. A
left extension
T
-pseudoalgebra
comprises

(5.13.(1))

an object
A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
;

(5.13.(2))

a 1-cell
f^{a}_{X}\colon TX\to A
for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
;

(5.13.(3))

an invertible 2-cell
\tilde{a}_{f}\colon f\Rightarrow f^{a}i_{X}
for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
,

such that

(5.13.(4))

for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the 2-cell
\tilde{a}_{f}
exhibits
f^{a}
as the left extension of
f
along
i_{X}
;

(5.13.(5))

for each
f\colon JX\to TY
and
g\colon TY\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the 2-cell
g^{a}\eta_{f}\colon g^{a}f\Rightarrow g^{a}f^{*}i_{X}
exhibits
g^{a}f^{*}
as the left extension of
g^{a}f
along
i_{X}
.

The following is an analogue of
[
FGHW18
, Theorem 5.3(i & v)]
for pseudoalgebras. That left extension
T
-pseudoalgebras induce
T
-pseudoalgebras is also proven in
[
Lew20
, Lemma 4.37]
.

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
T
be a relative pseudomonad. For each object
A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, there is a bijection between left extension
T
-pseudoalgebra structures on
A
and lax-idempotent
T
-pseudoalgebra structures on
A
.

Proof.

Given a left-extension
T
-pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
, and 1-cells
f\colon JX\to TY
and
g\colon JY\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the 2-cell
g^{a}\eta_{f}\colon g^{a}f\Rightarrow g^{a}f^{*}i_{X}
induces a 2-cell
\hat{a}_{g,f}\colon(g^{a}f)^{a}\Rightarrow g^{a}f^{*}
by the universal property of the left extension
(g^{a}f)^{a}
. The proof that this data satisfies the axioms of a
T
-pseudoalgebra follows as in the proof of
[
FGHW18
, Theorem 5.3(v
{\Rightarrow}
i)]
. That the
T
-pseudoalgebra is lax-idempotent follows from
Lemma

5.2
. The converse is trivial. That this correspondence is bijective follows from uniqueness of the universal property of the left extension defining
\hat{a}_{g,f}
.
∎

Remark 5.15
.

We may define a
T
-pseudoalgebra
for a left extension relative pseudomonad
T
in the sense of
[
FGHW18
, Definition 5.2]
8
8

8

[
FGHW18
]
uses the term
relative left Kan pseudomonad
, but we prefer a more descriptive term.
to simply be a left extension pseudoalgebra for the induced lax-idempotent relative pseudomonad
[
FGHW18
, Theorem 5.3]
.
However, note that the definition of left extension pseudoalgebra does not require the relative pseudomonad itself to be lax-idempotent.

We may also formulate the notion of pseudomorphism between left extension pseudoalgebras in terms of left extensions. (By
Theorem

5.7
, the appropriate notion of lax morphism for left extension pseudoalgebras is uninteresting, being simply a 1-cell between the carriers.)

Corollary 5.16
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor,
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad, and
(A,{-}^{a},\tilde{a})
and
(B,{-}^{b},\tilde{b})
be left extension
T
-pseudoalgebras. A 1-cell
h\colon A\to B
admits the (necessarily unique) structure of a pseudomorphism between the induced
T
-pseudoalgebras (
Proposition

5.14
) if and only if, for each
f\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the 2-cell
h\tilde{a}_{f}
exhibits
hf^{a}\colon TX\to B
as the left extension of
hf\colon JX\to B
along
i_{X}\colon JX\to TX
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Theorem

5.7
, the family of 2-cells exhibiting the unique lax morphism structure being precisely those induced by the universal property of the left extensions.
∎

5.2.
Lax fixed points

We conclude this section by discussing an aspect of lax-idempotence only present for non-relative pseudomonads. We do this for two reasons. The first is that our treatment of lax-idempotence for relative pseudomonads looks quite different from the usual treatments of lax-idempotent pseudomonads (e.g. in
[
Mar97
,
KL97
]
), and it is helpful to clarify the relationship. The second is that it explains why some properties of lax-idempotent pseudomonads do not appear to generalise to relative pseudomonads.

Recall from
Proposition

5.6
that a pseudomonad is lax-idempotent if and only if each of its pseudoalgebras is lax-idempotent.
There is an alternative characterisation of lax-idempotence that is more familiar in the literature on lax-idempotent pseudomonads. We recall this in
Proposition

5.19
after presenting a preliminary definition. In the following, we will continue to work with pseudomonads in extension form, i.e. as pseudomonads relative to the identity, rather than in pseudomonoid form.

Recall from
Remark

3.18
that, given a pseudoalgebra in extension form
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
, we obtain the usual pseudoalgebra structure 1-cell as
(1_{A})^{a}\colon TA\to A
.

Definition 5.17
.

Let
T
be a pseudomonad on a bicategory
{\mathcal{E}}
. A pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is a
lax fixed point
if
\tilde{a}_{1_{A}}^{-1}\colon(1_{A})^{a}\circ i_{A}\Rightarrow 1_{A}
exhibits the counit of an adjunction
(1_{A})^{a}\dashv i_{A}\colon A\to TA
.

Remark 5.18
.

Given a pseudomonad
T
and an object
A
for which
i_{A}\colon A\to TA
exhibits the right adjoint of a coreflection,
A
is automatically equipped with the structure of a colax
T
-algebra
[
Ště24
, Lemma 2.4.16]
. When
T
is lax-idempotent, every such colax structure is automatically pseudo
[
Ště24
, Proposition 2.5.11]
.

Note that the notion of lax fixed point does not make sense for relative pseudomonads, because it is not generally possibly to form
(1_{A})^{a}
unless
J
is the identity. Consequently, there is no analgoue of the following characterisation of lax-idempotent pseudomonads in the relative setting.

Proposition 5.19

(
[
Mar97
, Theorem 10.7]
)
.

A (non-relative) pseudomonad is lax-idempotent if and only if every pseudoalgebra is a lax fixed point.

Example 5.20
.

Propositions

5.6
and
5.19
together say that, given a pseudomonad
T
, every
T
-pseudoalgebra is lax-idempotent if and only if every
T
-pseudoalgebra is a fixed point. However, in general, these two properties do not coincide. For instance, let
T
be any pseudomonad on
\mathbf{CAT}
. Then the terminal category is necessarily a lax-idempotent
T
-pseudoalgebra (since in that case (
17
) is trivial), but is a lax fixed point if and only if
T(1)
admits a terminal object. In particular, taking
T
the free strict monoidal category 2-monad, we have that
T(0)=1
and that
T(1)
does not admit a terminal object, so this furthermore shows that it is not even true that free pseudoalgebras are lax-idempotent if and only if they are lax fixed points.

Lax fixed points admit a similar, but dual, characterisation to lax-idempotent pseudoalgebras in terms of lax morphisms (cf.
Theorem

5.7
); this observation appears to be new, as lax fixed points do not appear to have previously been studied for arbitrary pseudomonads, rather than lax-idempotent pseudomonads (although see
Remark

5.18
).

Theorem 5.21
.

Let
T
be a (non-relative) pseudomonad on a bicategory
{\mathcal{E}}
. A
T
-pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is a lax fixed point if and only if, for every
T
-pseudoalgebra
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
, every 1-cell
g\colon A\to B
in
{\mathcal{E}}
admits a unique lax morphism structure
\overline{g}
. Furthermore, in this case, the forgetful functor

U_{T}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T){\big[(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a}),(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})\big]}\to{\mathcal{E}}[A,B]

given by the action of the strict pseudofunctor
U_{T}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}_{l}(T)\to{\mathcal{E}}
is fully faithful.

Proof.

(
\implies
) Suppose
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is a lax fixed point. That (1)
g
admits a unique lax morphism structure, and (2) that every 2-cell between lax morphisms is a transformation both follow from the proof of
[
Ště24
, Lemma 2.4.17]
, observing that the proof goes through even without the assumption that the codomain is induced by an adjunction.

(
\impliedby
) Taking
g\mathrel{:=}i_{A}
, we have a candidate 2-cell for the unit as follows.

(20)

\nu\mathrel{:=}\Big(1_{TA}\xrightarrow{\theta_{A}^{-1}}(i_{A})^{*}\xrightarrow{\cong}(i_{A}1_{A})^{*}\xrightarrow{\overline{i_{A}}_{1_{A}}}i_{A}(1_{A})^{a}\Big)

To verify that
\nu
and
\tilde{a}_{1_{A}}^{-1}
define the unit and counit respectively of an adjunction
(1_{A})^{a}\dashv i_{A}
, we must show the two triangle laws. The right triangle law is straightforward once the definitions are unfolded, as shown below. The cell marked (
*
) is
[
FGHW18
, Lemma 3.2(iii)]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{TA}i_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{A})^{*}i_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{A}1_{A})^{*}i_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{A}(1_{A})^{a}i_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{A}1_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{A}^{-1}i_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i_{A}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{i}_{1_{A}}i_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i_{A}1_{A}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{A}\tilde{a}_{1_{A}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ast)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\eta}
i
unit.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu i_{A}}

For the left triangle law, we use the uniqueness of lax morphism structures on 1-cells out of
A
. Recall from
Corollary

3.20
that
(1_{A})^{a}
becomes a pseudomorphism by taking

\hat{a}_{1_{A},k}\colon(1_{A}k)^{a}\Rightarrow(1_{A})^{a}k^{*}

for each
k\colon JX\to A
. We thus have a composite lax morphism

(i_{A},\overline{i_{A}})\circ((1_{A})^{a},\hat{a})\colon(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})\to(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})

whose 2-cell component is given at
k\colon JX\to A
by the following composite:

(21)

\big((1_{A})^{a}i_{A}k\big)^{a}\xrightarrow{\hat{a}_{1_{A},i_{A}k}}(1_{A})^{a}(i_{A}k)^{*}\xrightarrow{(1_{A})^{a}\overline{i_{A}}_{k}}(1_{A})^{a}i_{A}k^{a}

On the other hand,
\tilde{a}_{1_{A}}\colon 1_{A}\xrightarrow{\cong}(1_{A})^{a}i_{A}
is an isomorphism in
{\mathcal{E}}
, so by the amnestic isofibration property of
U_{T}
(
Proposition

3.13
), we get a lax morphism structure on
(1_{A})^{a}i_{A}
induced from that on
1_{A}
. Explicitly, we have that the following defines a lax morphism structure on
(1_{A})^{a}i_{A}
for each
k\colon JX\to A
in
{\mathcal{E}}
:

(22)

\big((1_{A})^{a}i_{A}k\big)^{a}\xrightarrow{(\tilde{a}_{1_{A}}^{-1}k)^{a}}(1_{A}k)^{a}\xrightarrow{\cong}1_{A}k^{a}\xrightarrow{\tilde{a}_{1_{A}}k^{a}}(1_{A})^{a}i_{A}k^{a}

Since we have defined two lax morphism structures on
(1_{A})^{a}i_{A}
, we must have that (
21
)
=
(
22
). Denoting this fact by (
*
) for
k=1_{A}
, the triangle law follows from commutativity of the next diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{A})^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{A})^{a}1_{TA}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{A})^{a}(i_{A})^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{A})^{a}(i_{A}1_{A})^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{A})^{a}i_{A}(1_{A})^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big((1_{A})^{a}i_{A}\big)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big((1_{A})^{a}i_{A}1_{A}\big)^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{A}(1_{A})^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{A})^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{A}1_{A})^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1_{A})^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{A})^{a}\theta_{A}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{1_{A},i_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{A})^{a}\overline{i_{A}}_{1_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{1_{A},i_{A}1_{A}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ast)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{1_{A}}^{-1}(1_{A})^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{a}_{1_{A}}^{-1})^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{a}_{1_{A}}^{-1}1_{A})^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
A
unit.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\hat{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{A})^{a}\nu}

∎

The above characterisation is fundamental in establishing the following useful property of lax-idempotent pseudomonads, which in particular implies they are
fully property-like
in the sense of
[
KL97
, §5]
.

Proposition 5.22
.

Let
T
be a pseudomonad, let
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
and
(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
be pseudoalgebras, and let
f\colon A\to B
be a 1-cell in
{\mathcal{E}}
. Suppose that
f
admits a colax morphism structure. If
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is a lax fixed point then the canonical lax morphism structure of
Theorem

5.21
is inverse to the colax morphism structure.

Proof.

The proof follows that of
[
KL97
, Lemma 6.5]
exactly, observing that the full strength of the assumptions ibid. is not necessary. The key observation is that, since
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is a lax fixed point, there is an adjunction
{\mathcal{E}}[TA,B]\colon{({-})}\circ i_{A}\dashv{({-})}\circ(1_{A})^{a}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\mskip 2.0mu{\mathcal{E}}[A,B]
and so equality of 2-cells between 1-cells
TA\to B
may be tested by precomposing
i_{A}
. The unit laws for the lax and colax morphisms then imply that the given lax and colax morphism structures are inverse to one another.
∎

As a consequence of the characterisation theorem above, we recover the fact that colax morphisms of pseudoalgebras for lax-idempotent non-relative pseudomonads are automatically pseudomorphisms.

Corollary 5.23

(
[
KL97
, Lemma 6.5 & Corollary 6.6]
)
.

Every colax morphism between pseudoalgebras for a lax-idempotent pseudomonad is pseudo. Consequently, every 2-cell between colax morphisms is a transformation.

Proof.

By
Proposition

5.19
, every pseudoalgebra for a lax-idempotent pseudomonad is a lax fixed point. By
Theorem

5.21
, every 1-cell between pseudoalgebras admits a unique lax morphism structure, for which every 2-cell is a transformation. The first claim thus follows from
Proposition

5.22
. The second claim then follows immediately, because a 2-cell is a transformation for a pseudomorphism qua colax morphism if and only if it is a transformation for a pseudomorphism qua lax morphism.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

We do not know whether an analogue of
Corollary

5.23
holds for relative pseudomonads. As explained above, the proof strategy in the non-relative setting crucially relies on the fact that pseudoalgebras for lax-idempotent pseudomonads are lax fixed points (rather than the fact they are lax-idempotent) and so does not extend to the relative setting. However, we also do not know of an example of a colax morphism between lax-idempotent
T
-pseudoalgebras, for
T
a relative pseudomonad, that is not a pseudomorphism.

6.
Resolutions of relative pseudomonads and coherence

In the one-dimensional setting, the category of algebras for a relative monad
T
satisfies a universal property among resolutions of
T
(that is, relative adjunctions inducing
T
)
[
ACU15
, Theorem 2.12]
, generalising that for monads
[
EM65
, Theorem 2.2]
. In the two-dimensional setting, the 2-category of strict algebras for a relative 2-monad satisfies an analogous universal property
[
AM24
, Corollary 6.41]
. In this section, we establish the appropriate universal property in the non-strict setting. In fact, we shall establish a universal property not just for the bicategory of pseudoalgebras, but also for the Kleisli bicategory, as the universal property of the latter was merely alluded to in
[
FGHW18
, Remark 4.5]
. As a consequence, we shall deduce that the Kleisli bicategory embeds essentially fully faithfully into the bicategory of pseudoalgebras, before discussing some consequences.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad. We define a 2-category
{\mathrm{Res}}(T)
as follows.

•

An object is a
resolution
of
T
: a
J
-relative pseudoadjunction for which the induced relative pseudomonad is exactly
T
. Where there is no risk of ambiguity, we shall typically denote a resolution simply by its apex.

•

A 1-cell from
(L\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{C}},R\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{E}},i,\sharp,\eta,\varepsilon)
to
(L\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},R\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{E}},i^{\prime},\sharp^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
is a
morphism
of resolutions: a pseudofunctor
M\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
for which
R=R^{\prime}M
and for which
MLX=L^{\prime}X
for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
.

•

A 2-cell from
M
to
M^{\prime}
is a
transformation
of resolutions: a pseudonatural identity
9
9

9

A
pseudonatural identity
is a pseudonatural transformation whose components are identities.

\varpi\colon M\Rightarrow M^{\prime}
such that
R^{\prime}(\varpi_{f})=1_{Rf}
.

The assignment sending each resolution to its apex defines a 2-functor from
{\mathrm{Res}}(T)
to the 2-category of bicategories, pseudofunctors, and pseudonatural identities, which is faithful on 1-cells and 2-cells (cf.
[
Lac10a
, Theorem 3.2]
).

Definition

6.1
defines the notion of resolution in the strictest possible sense, rather than up to a notion of equivalence. This is because we are interested in universal properties with respect to a fixed relative pseudomonad. Note that, as observed in
[
Mir23
, Proposition 2.2.1(7)]
, an analogous notion of resolution for a (non-relative) pseudomonad in pseudomonoid form (i.e. in terms of a multiplication operator
\mu\colon TT\Rightarrow T
rather than an extension operator) would be too strict: given a pseudomonad
T
, the composite pseudofunctor for the Kleisli pseudoadjunction sends a 1-cell
f\colon X\to Y
to the 1-cell

TX\xrightarrow{Tf}TY\xrightarrow{Ti_{Y}}TTY\xrightarrow{m_{Y}}TY

which is not equal to
Tf\colon TX\to TY
, but only isomorphic via one of the unitors for
T
. Consequently, an advantage of working with pseudomonads in extension form (i.e. as pseudomonads relative to an identity) is that the Kleisli pseudoadjunction induces
T
strictly (cf.
[
FGHW18
, Remark 4.5]
). This is because, in the relative setting, the pseudofunctoriality of
T
is derived rather than extra structure.

The definition of a morphism of resolutions may appear too weak, as it does not require all of the structure of a relative pseudoadjunction to be preserved; as the following lemma shows, this is in fact automatic (for organisational convenience, we make reference to the subsequent
Lemma

6.9
, while noting that this does not depend on any of the preceding material).

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
(L\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{C}},R\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{E}},i,\sharp,\eta,\varepsilon)
and
(L\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},R\colon{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{E}},i^{\prime},\sharp^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
be resolutions of a given relative pseudomonad and let
M\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
be a morphism of resolutions.

(6.2.(1))

The pseudonatural transformations
i
and
i^{\prime}
are equal, the modifications
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
are equal, and the following two natural transformations are equal, pseudonaturally in
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}[LX,C]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}[L^{\prime}X,MC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,R^{\prime}MC]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp_{X,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{LX,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{LX,C}{({-})}i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}_{L^{\prime}X,MC}{({-})}i^{\prime}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{X,C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,R^{\prime}MC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}[L^{\prime}X,MC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,R^{\prime}MC]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp^{\prime}_{X,MC}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}_{L^{\prime}X,MC}{({-})}i^{\prime}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}_{X,MC}}

(6.2.(2))

The following diagram commutes, pseudonaturally in
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
and

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}[LX,C]}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}[L^{\prime}X,MC]}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{LX,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp^{\prime}_{X,MC}}

(6.2.(3))

The pseudofunctors
ML
and

are equal.

(6.2.(4))

The following two natural transformations are equal, pseudonaturally in
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
and

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}[LX,C]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}[LX,C]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}[L^{\prime}X,MC]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{LX,C}{({-})}i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp_{X,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{LX,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{X,C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}[LX,C]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}[L^{\prime}X,MC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,R^{\prime}MC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}[L^{\prime}X,MC]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{LX,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}_{L^{\prime}X,MC}{({-})}i^{\prime}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp^{\prime}_{X,MC}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\prime}_{X,MC}}

(6.2.(5))

For each
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the canonical pseudoalgebra structure on
R(C)
induced by
Lemma

6.9
is equal to the canonical pseudoalgebra structure on
R^{\prime}(MC)
.
10
10

10

The analogous condition in dimension one was taken as an assumption in
[

, Theorem 2.12]
, but is automatic, as observed in
[

, Lemma 5.26]
.
Furthermore, for each 1-cell
h\colon C\to D
in
{\mathcal{C}}
, the canonical pseudomorphism structure on
R(h)
induced by
Lemma

6.9
is equal to the canonical pseudomorphism structure on
R(Mh)
.

Proof.

(6.2.(1))

By the definition of a resolution, the units of the relative pseudomonads induced by both relative pseudoadjunctions are equal, hence induce identical pseudonatural transformations; and similarly for the modifications. The equality of the two induced natural transformations is then trivial.

(6.2.(2))

We have the following pasting diagram, where the middle square commutes since
M
is a morphism of resolutions.

(23)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}[LX,C]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}[L^{\prime}X,MC]}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,R^{\prime}MC]}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp_{X,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{LX,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{LX,C}{({-})}i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\prime}_{L^{\prime}X,MC}{({-})}i^{\prime}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp^{\prime}_{X,MC}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{X,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{\prime}_{X,MC}}

Using

6.2.(1)
, and the right triangle law for
\eta^{\prime}
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
, this 2-cell is equal to the identity.

for each

. We also have that the following diagram commutes pseudonaturally in

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{A}}[X,Y]}}

By (

), the top composite defines
L_{X,Y}\colon{\mathcal{A}}[X,Y]\to{\mathcal{C}}[LX,LY]
, while the bottom composite defines

.

This follows from

, pasting

on the left-hand side of (

), and then using the right triangle law.

The following diagram commutes: the left square by

Hence the induced pseudoalgebras have the same extension operator.
It remains to show that the associativity (

) and unitality structure (

) of the induced pseudoalgebras are equal: the former follows from

and

; the latter follows trivially from

.

That the pseudomorphism structures on

and

coincide follows from the pseudonaturality of

together with the right pseudoadjoint compatibility condition for
