.
∎

6.1.
The bi-initial resolution

We now show that the Kleisli bicategory for a relative pseudomonad
T
admits a universal property with respect to resolutions of
T
. For non-relative pseudomonads, this was alluded to in
[
CHP03
, Corollary 4.4]
, but does not appear to have been explicitly proven.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor and let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad. The Kleisli resolution is bi-initial in
{\mathrm{Res}}(T)
.

Proof.

Let
(L,R,i,\sharp,\eta,\varepsilon)
be a resolution of
T
with apex
{\mathcal{C}}
. We construct a mediating pseudofunctor
{!}_{L,R}\colon\mathrm{Kl}(T)\to{\mathcal{C}}
as follows.

\displaystyle|{!}_{L,R}|

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}|L|

\displaystyle({!}_{L,R})_{X,Y}

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\sharp_{X,LX}\colon\mathrm{Kl}(T)[X,Y]={\mathcal{E}}[JX,RLX]\to{\mathcal{C}}[LX,LY]

Since
(L,R)
is a resolution of
T
, we have
g^{*}=R(g^{\sharp})
. Thus, given 1-cells
f\colon JX\to TY
and
g\colon JY\to TZ
in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the unit of the adjoint equivalence, the compositor of
R
, and the counit of the adjoint equivalence provide an invertible natural transformation:

(24)

(g^{*}f)^{\sharp}=(R(g^{\sharp})f)^{\sharp}\xrightarrow{\eta}(R(g^{\sharp})R(f^{\sharp})i_{X})^{\sharp}\xrightarrow{\cong}(R(g^{\sharp}f^{\sharp})i_{X})^{\sharp}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}g^{\sharp}f^{\sharp}

A unitor of
{\mathcal{E}}
, the unitor of
R
, and the counit of the adjoint equivalence defining the pseudoadjunction provide an invertible natural transformation:

(25)

{i_{X}}^{\sharp_{X,LX}}\xrightarrow{(\cong)^{\sharp_{X,LX}}}(1_{RLX}\circ i_{X})^{\sharp_{X,LX}}\xrightarrow{(\cong)^{\sharp_{X,LX}}}(R(1_{LX})\circ i_{X})^{\sharp_{X,LX}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{X,LX}}1_{LX}

The proof that these data define a pseudofunctor is directly analogous to the proof that
V_{T}\colon\mathrm{Kl}(T)\to{\mathcal{E}}
forms a pseudofunctor (
[
FGHW18
, Lemma 4.3]
), noting that the relevant structural isomorphisms
\mu
and
\theta
are induced by (
24
) and (
25
) respectively (cf.
[
FGHW18
, Theorem 3.8]
).

{!}_{L,R}\colon\mathrm{Kl}(T)\to{\mathcal{C}}
forms a morphism of resolutions of
T
: the compatibility condition for the left pseudoadjoints is immediate from the definition of
|{!}_{L,R}|
, while the compatibility condition for the right pseudoadjoints follows from the fact that
(L,R,i,\sharp,\eta,\varepsilon)
is a resolution of
T
.

It remains to show that every morphism of resolutions is uniquely isomorphic to
{!}_{L,R}
. Let
M\colon\mathrm{Kl}(T)\to{\mathcal{C}}
be a morphism of resolutions. Since
|\mathrm{Kl}(T)|=|{\mathcal{A}}|
, the compatibility condition for the left pseudoadjoints implies that

(26)

M(X)=M(K_{T}(X))=L(X)={!}_{L,R}(X)

for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
. Now, for each 1-cell
f\colon JX\to TY
, the compatibility condition for the right pseudoadjoints states that
R(M(f))=V_{T}(f)=f^{*}=R(f^{\sharp})
. Therefore, we have an invertible 2-cell

(27)

f^{\sharp}\xrightarrow{(\eta_{f})^{\sharp}}(R(f^{\sharp})i_{X})^{\sharp}=(R(M(f))i_{X})^{\sharp}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{Mf}}Mf

natural in
f
, defining a pseudonatural identity
{!}_{L,R}\Rightarrow M
. By
Item

6.2.(4)
,
\varepsilon_{Mf}=M(\eta_{f}^{-1})
, and so applying
R
to (
27
) gives the identity, as does applying
R
to the inverse of (
27
). Consequently, (
27
) defines an invertible transformation of resolutions.

Finally, let
\varpi\colon{!}_{L,R}\Rightarrow{!}_{L,R}
be a transformation of resolutions, so that, for each 1-cell
{f\colon JX\to RLY}
, we have an invertible 2-cell on the left below, for which the 2-cell on the right below is the identity.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{f}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces JX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RLX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RLY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces JX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RLX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RLY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(f^{\sharp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(f^{\sharp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\varpi_{f})}

Now, since
\sharp_{X,RL}
is functorial, applying it to the 2-cell above right gives the identity 2-cell. By pre- and postcomposing by the isomorphism
R(f^{\sharp})^{\sharp}\cong f^{\sharp}
, we get that
\varpi_{f}
is the identity. Consequently,
{!}_{L,R}
has no nontrivial endomorphisms, which implies the uniqueness of the isomorphisms of morphisms of resolutions.
∎

We may use
Theorem

6.3
to give a characterisation of those pseudoadjunctions that exhibit the Kleisli resolution: this is dual in a certain sense to the question of relative pseudomonadicity, and might thus be called a
relative pseudo-opmonadicity theorem
. For relative monads, this appears as
[
Ark22
, Proposition 3.1.11]
, and for non-relative pseudomonads, appears as
[
Mir23
, Theorem 4.2.2]
.

Recall that a pseudofunctor
F\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
is
essentially surjective
if, for each object
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
, there exists an object
Y\in{\mathcal{B}}
such that
F(X)\simeq Y
; and is
essentially fully faithful
if, for each pair of objects
X,Y\in{\mathcal{A}}
, the functor
F_{X,Y}\colon{\mathcal{A}}[X,Y]\to{\mathcal{B}}[FX,FY]
is an equivalence.

Theorem 6.4
.

A relative pseudoadjunction exhibits the Kleisli resolution of a relative pseudomonad, up to biequivalence, if and only if the left pseudoadjoint is essentially surjective on objects.

Proof.

By definition,
K_{T}\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathrm{Kl}(T)
is the identity on objects (
Theorem

2.13
). Conversely, suppose that the left pseudoadjoint
L\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
of a relative pseudoadjunction is essentially surjective on objects. Writing
T
for the induced pseudomonad, there is an essentially fully faithful pseudofunctor
!_{L,R}\colon\mathrm{Kl}(T)\to{\mathcal{C}}
by
Theorem

6.3
. By assumption, and the fact that
K_{T}
is identity-on-objects, this pseudofunctor is also essentially surjective on objects. Thus
!_{L,R}
is a biequivalence
[
JY21
, Theorem 7.4.1]
.
∎

We shall use the relative pseudo-opmonadicity theorem to give an alternative characterisation of the Kleisli bicategory for a relative pseudomonad.

Lemma 6.5
.

Every pseudofunctor
F\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
admits a unique factorisation, the
full image factorisation
11
11

11

The full image factorisation of a pseudofunctor was called the
Gabriel factorization
of a pseudofunctor in
[
GJ17
]
.
, into an identity-on-objects pseudofunctor followed by a fully faithful pseudofunctor.

{\mathcal{A}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Im}(F)\rightarrowtail{\mathcal{B}}

Furthermore, if
{\mathcal{B}}
is a 2-category, then so is
\mathrm{Im}(F)
.

Proof.

The bicategory
\mathrm{Im}(F)
has the same objects as
{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathrm{Im}(F)[X,Y]\mathrel{:=}{\mathcal{B}}[FX,FY]
, with the bicategory structure being inherited from
{\mathcal{B}}
. The pseudofunctor
{\mathcal{A}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Im}(F)
is given on hom-categories by
F
; whereas the (strict) pseudofunctor
\mathrm{Im}(F)\rightarrowtail{\mathcal{B}}
is given on objects by
F
. Uniqueness follows by definition.
∎

Extending an observation in
[
Ark22
, §6.1]
, taking the full image factorisation of a left relative pseudoadjoint produces a new relative pseudoadjunction inducing the same relative pseudomonad.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad and let
(L,R,i,\sharp,\eta,\varepsilon)
be a resolution of
T
. Denote by
IL^{\prime}=L
the full image factorisation of
L
. Then:

(6.6.(1))

X\mapsto L^{\prime}X
defines a left
J
-relative pseudoadjoint to
RI
;

(6.6.(2))

the corresponding relative pseudoadjunction is a resolution of
T
;

(6.6.(3))

I
is a morphism of resolutions, and is the unique such.

Proof.

The unit of the relative pseudoadjunction is given by
i
, and the adjoint equivalence is given by the following.

{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RIY]\xrightarrow{{({-})}^{\sharp}_{X,IY}}{\mathcal{C}}[IL^{\prime}X,IY]=\mathrm{Im}(L)[L^{\prime}X,Y]

It is trivial that this defines a resolution of
T
. That
I
defines a morphism of resolutions is by definition of the factorisation. Finally, any morphism of resolutions
I^{\prime}
must satisfy
I^{\prime}L^{\prime}=L
by
Item

6.2.(3)
, which implies uniqueness given that
L^{\prime}
is the full image.
∎

Consequently, every resolution of a relative pseudomonad gives an alternative presentation of the Kleisli bicategory.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad and let
(L\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{C}},R\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{E}},i,\sharp,\eta,\varepsilon)
be a resolution of
T
. Then
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
is biequivalent to
\mathrm{Im}(L)
.

Proof.

Immediate from
Theorem

6.4
, since
{\mathcal{A}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Im}(L)
is identity-on-objects by definition and forms a resolution of
T
by
Proposition

6.6
.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

A word of caution: while
\mathrm{Im}(L)
forms a resolution of
T
and is biequivalent to
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
, it is not equivalent to
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
as an object of
{\mathrm{Res}}(T)
. A morphism of resolutions from
\mathrm{Im}(L)
to
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
should send each 1-cell
f\colon LX\to LY
to
Rf\circ i_{X}\colon JX\to TY
in
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
. However, this does not satisfy the required compatibility condition for the right pseudoadjoints, since
Rf
is merely isomorphic to
V_{T}(Rf\circ i_{X})=(Rf\circ i_{X})^{*}
. One could presumably rectify this by working with
pseudomorphisms
of resolutions, which require the pseudoadjoints to commute only up to coherent pseudonatural isomorphism, but we shall have no need to do so here.

6.2.
The 2-terminal resolution

We now turn to the universal property of the bicategory of pseudoalgebras. First, we observe that right pseudoadjoints exhibit pseudoalgebras in the following sense.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
(L,R,i,\sharp,\eta,\varepsilon)
be a
J
-relative pseudoadjunction inducing a
J
-relative pseudomonad
T
.

(6.9.(1))

For each object
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the object
RC\in{\mathcal{E}}
is canonically equipped with the structure of a
T
-pseudoalgebra.

(6.9.(2))

For each 1-cell
h\colon C\to D
in
{\mathcal{C}}
, the 1-cell
Rh\colon RC\to RD
in
{\mathcal{E}}
is canonically equipped with the structure of a pseudomorphism.

(6.9.(3))

For each 2-cell
\alpha\colon h\Rightarrow h^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{C}}
, the 2-cell
R\alpha\colon Rh\Rightarrow Rh^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{E}}
forms a transformation.

Proof.

Given an object
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, we define an extension operator by the following assignment:

(28)

{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RC]\xrightarrow{\sharp_{X,C}}{\mathcal{C}}[LX,C]\xrightarrow{R_{LX,C}}{\mathcal{E}}[RLX,RC]

Observing that, since
(L,R)
is a resolution of
T
, we have
f^{*}=R(f^{\sharp})
and hence
R(g^{\sharp})R(f^{\sharp})=R(g^{\sharp})f^{*}
, the associativity structure is given by the following:

(29)

R((R(g^{\sharp})f)^{\sharp})\xrightarrow{R((R(g^{\sharp})\eta)^{\sharp})}R((R(g^{\sharp})R(f^{\sharp})i_{X})^{\sharp})\xrightarrow{\cong}R((R(g^{\sharp}f^{\sharp})i_{X})^{\sharp})\xrightarrow{R(\varepsilon)}R(g^{\sharp}f^{\sharp})\xrightarrow{\cong}R(g^{\sharp})R(f^{\sharp})

and the unitality structure is given by the unit of the adjoint equivalence:

(30)

f\xrightarrow{\eta_{f}}R(f^{\sharp})i_{X}

The proof is directly analogous to the proof that relative pseudoadjunctions induce relative pseudomonads (
[
FGHW18
, Theorem 3.8]
), noting that the data of the pseudoalgebra (
28
), (
29
), and (
30
) correspond respectively to the data
{({-})}^{*}
,
\mu
, and
\eta
of the relative pseudomonad induced by the relative pseudoadjunction.

Given a 1-cell
h\colon C\to D
in
{\mathcal{C}}
, the pseudomorphism structure on
Rh\colon RC\to RD
is given by the following invertible 2-cell, using pseudonaturality of
\sharp
and pseudofunctoriality of
R
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[JX,RD]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}[LX,C]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{C}}[LX,D]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[RLX,RC]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathcal{E}}[RLX,RD]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp_{X,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{LX,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp_{X,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{LX,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}[RLX,Rh]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}[JX,Rh]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}[LX,h]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

That this defines a pseudomorphism follows from the adjoint equivalence
{({-})}^{\sharp}\dashv R{({-})}i
. That, for each 2-cell
\alpha\colon h\Rightarrow h^{\prime}
, the 2-cell
R\alpha\colon Rh\Rightarrow Rh^{\prime}
forms a transformation follows from pseudonaturality of
\sharp
.
∎

As anticipated in the introduction, the resolution associated to the bicategory of pseudoalgebras (
Theorem

3.21
) admits a universal property dual to that of the Kleisli bicategory (in fact, it is stricter).

Theorem 6.10
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor and let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad. The pseudoalgebra resolution is 2-terminal in
{\mathrm{Res}}(T)
.

Proof.

Let
(L,R,i,\sharp,\eta,\varepsilon)
be a resolution of
T
. We construct a pseudofunctor
{!}_{L,R}\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
. The action of
{!}_{L,R}
is given by equipping each object
RC\in{\mathcal{E}}
, for
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, with the canonical pseudoalgebra structure of
Lemma

6.9
, and likewise for the 1-cells and 2-cells. Pseudofunctoriality of
{!}_{L,R}
follows from that of
R
. The pseudofunctor
!_{L,R}
forms a morphism of resolutions: the compatibility condition for the left pseudoadjoints follows since
(L,R,i,\sharp)
is a resolution of
T
and
!_{L,R}
is defined in terms of the canonical pseudoalgebra structure on
R
, so that the pseudoalgebra structure on each
RLX
is precisely the free pseudoalgebra structure on
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
given by
Proposition

3.19
; the compatibility condition for the right pseudoadjoints follows trivially.

It remains to show that every morphism of resolutions is equal to
!_{L,R}
. Let
M\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
be a morphism of resolutions. From
Theorems

3.21
and
6.2.(5)
, we have that, for each
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the pseudoalgebra structure on
U_{T}MC
is equal to the canonical pseudoalgebra structure on
RC
, and that, for each 1-cell
h\colon C\to D
in
{\mathcal{C}}
, the pseudomorphism structure on
U_{T}Mh
is equal to the canonical pseudomorphism structure on
Rh
. Since
U_{T}
is locally faithful (
Proposition

3.13
), this shows that
{!}_{L,R}=M
, which is automatically a transformation of resolutions.

Finally, let
\varpi\colon!_{L,R}\Rightarrow!_{L,R}
be a transformation of resolutions. We have that
U_{T}(\varpi_{f})=1_{Rf}
. Since
U_{T}
is locally faithful, this implies that
\varpi_{f}
is the identity.
∎

A resolution of a relative pseudomonad
T
will be called
relatively pseudomonadic
when the unique mediating pseudofunctor into the bicategory of pseudoalgebras is a biequivalence.

As a consequence of
Theorems

6.3
and
6.10
, we deduce the existence of a canonical
comparison pseudofunctor
from the Kleisli bicategory to the bicategory of algebras.

Corollary 6.11
.

There is a unique, fully faithful pseudofunctor
I_{T}\colon\mathrm{Kl}(T)\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
such that the following diagrams commute: explicitly,
I_{T}
is the embedding of the free
T
-pseudoalgebras.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Kl}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{T}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Kl}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{T}}

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of such a pseudofunctor follows from
Theorem

6.10
:
I_{T}={!}_{F_{T},U_{T}}
is the unique mediating pseudofunctor induced by the resolution associated to the bicategory of
T
-pseudoalgebras; full faithfulness follows from
Theorem

6.3
. Explicitly, the action of
I_{T}
is induced by the pseudofunctor
F_{T}
, which sends objects of
{\mathcal{A}}
to free
T
-pseudoalgebras.
∎

6.3.
Relative pseudomonads induced by pseudomonads

We use the universal properties exhibited above to make some observations regarding relative pseudomonads induced from pseudomonads by precomposition; these observations will be useful in
Section

7
when we study the relationship between the presheaf construction and the small presheaf construction.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor.

(6.12.(1))

Let
T
be a pseudomonad on
{\mathcal{E}}
. The assignment
(A\in{\mathcal{A}})\mapsto(TJA\in{\mathcal{E}})
extends to a
J
-relative monad.

(6.12.(2))

Let
R\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor admitting a left pseudoadjoint
L\colon{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
. The assignment
(A\in{\mathcal{A}})\mapsto(LJA\in{\mathcal{C}})
defines a left
J
-relative adjoint to
R
.

Furthermore, if
(L,R)
is a resolution of
T
, then
(LJ,R)
is a resolution of
TJ
.

Proof.

It is trivial to check that the data of a pseudomonad and pseudoadjunction (viewed as being relative to the identity pseudofunctor on
{\mathcal{E}}
) restrict along
J
as described.
∎

Given a pseudofunctor
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
and a pseudomonad
T
on its codomain, it is natural to compare the Kleisli bicategories and bicategories of pseudoalgebras for
T
and
TJ
. First, the Kleisli bicatgory for
TJ
may be characterised simply in terms of that for
T
.

Corollary 6.13
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor and let
T
be a pseudomonad on
E
. The Kleisli bicategory
\mathrm{Kl}(TJ)
is biequivalent to the full image of the composite
{\mathcal{A}}\xrightarrow{J}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{K_{T}}\mathrm{Kl}(T)
. Consequently, the full image of the comparison pseudofunctor
!_{TJ}\colon\mathrm{Kl}(TJ)\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(TJ)
is biequivalent to
\mathrm{Im}(F_{T}J)
.

Proof.

Both claims follows immediately from
Lemmas

6.12
and
6.7
, together with
Theorems

2.13
and
3.21
respectively.
∎

The bicategory of pseudoalgebras for
T
is not in general biequivalent to that for
TJ
(as is easily seen, for instance, by taking
J
to be the unique pseudofunctor from the empty bicategory). However, there is a canonical pseudofunctor from the former to the latter.

Corollary 6.14
.

Let
T
be a pseudomonad and let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor. There is a unique pseudofunctor
!_{J}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(TJ)
that commutes with the free and forgetful pseudofunctors.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{PsAlg}(TJ)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{T}J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{TJ}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{PsAlg}(TJ)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{TJ}}

Proof.

By
Lemma

6.12
, the following relative pseudoadjunction forms a resolution of
TJ
, after which the result follows from
Theorem

6.10
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{T}J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

∎

6.4.
Coherence

We end this section with an observation regarding an aspect of the embedding of free pseudoalgebras that does not arise in the one-dimensional setting. As mentioned in
Remark

3.11
, when
{\mathcal{E}}
is a 2-category, so too is
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
. However, the same is not true for
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
, which is a bicategory regardless of the strictness of the domain or codomain. The comparison pseudofunctor
I_{T}\colon\mathrm{Kl}(T)\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
therefore provides an essentially fully faithful embedding of a bicategory into a 2-category. It is well known that every bicategory is biequivalent to a 2-category
[
MP85
,
Pow89
]
. However, in general, the 2-category to which a given bicategory is biequivalent may not admit a convenient direct description. For instance, syntactic approaches to coherence give a description in terms of free 2-categories, whereas an approach via the bicategorical Yoneda embedding gives a description in terms of pseudopresheaves
[
GPS95
, Theorem 1.4]
. Yet it is known that, somewhat surprisingly, in many cases naturally occurring bicategories do admit a natural strictification, in that there exists a biequivalent 2-category that arises by natural considerations, rather than for the purposes of strictification (cf.
[
Lac10
, §1.5]
). Our contention is that this tends to occur due to the following consequence of the following observation, which may be viewed as a coherence theorem associated to a relative pseudomonad, and does not appear to have been explicitly stated even for non-relative pseudomonads.

Corollary 6.15
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor and let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad. Suppose that
{\mathcal{E}}
is a 2-category. The bicategory
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
is biequivalent to
\mathrm{Im}(F_{T})
, the full and locally full sub-2-category of
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
spanned by the free
T
-pseudoalgebras.

Proof.

From
Remarks

3.11
and
6.11
.
∎

We shall give several applications of
Corollary

6.15
in
Section

7.3
.

7.
Presheaves and cocompleteness

In
[
FGHW18
, Example 4.2]
, it is established that the Kleisli bicategory
\mathrm{Kl}(P)
associated to the presheaf construction is the bicategory
{\mathbf{Dist}}
of distributors (a.k.a. the bicategory
\mathbf{Prof}
of profunctors) between small categories (cf.
[
Bén73
]
). The objective of this section is to characterise the 2-category
\mathrm{PsAlg}(P)
of pseudoalgebras and pseudomorphisms for the presheaf construction. In contrast to characterising the Kleisli bicategory, this is not a straightforward task. To illustrate why, we contrast it with a closely related construction, which we now recall.

Definition 7.1

(
[
Ulm68
,
Kel82
,
DL07
]
)
.

The
small presheaf construction

A\mapsto[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]_{s}
sends a locally small category
A
to the full subcategory of
[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
spanned by presheaves that are small colimits of representables. This defines a pseudomonad
P_{s}
on the 2-category
\mathbf{CAT}
of locally small categories.

Characterising the pseudoalgebras for
P_{s}
is straightforward: since
P_{s}
is a lax-idempotent pseudomonad, a locally small category
A
admits the structure of a a
P_{s}
-pseudoalgebra if and only if the unit
A\to P_{s}(A)
admits a left adjoint (
Remark

5.18
). Every such category is reflective in a cocomplete category, so is itself cocomplete; conversely, every cocomplete category admits such a left adjoint via left extension. Consequently, a locally small category admits the structure of a
P_{s}
-pseudoalgebra if and only if it admits all small colimits. Similarly, the pseudomorphisms are precisely the cocontinuous functors, and the transformations are the natural transformations.

Following
Lemma

6.12
, we may precompose any pseudomonad on
\mathbf{CAT}
with the inclusion
J\colon\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
. Applying this construction to the small presheaf construction recovers the presheaf construction (
Example

2.8
): in other words,
P=P_{s}J
.

In light of this observation, one might expect it to be similarly simple to prove that, like the
P_{s}
-pseudoalgebras, the
P
-pseudoalgebras are precisely the cocomplete locally small categories. However, there is a crucial difference. While
P
is a lax-idempotent relative pseudomonad
[
FGHW18
, Example 5.4]
, the pseudoalgebras for such do not admit a convenient characterisation in terms of reflectivity in a free pseudoalgebra, which was crucial for characterising the
P_{s}
-pseudoalgebras. Furthermore, although lax-idempotent pseudoalgebras
do
admit a simpler description than arbitrary pseudoalgebras by
Proposition

5.14
, the pseudoalgebras for a lax-idempotent relative pseudomonad are not necessarily also lax-idempotent (
Example

5.5
). Since we do not know a priori that the
P
-pseudoalgebras are lax-idempotent, we cannot use this description.

Consequently, a characterisation of the
P
-pseudoalgebras necessitates a fundamentally different proof strategy to the characterisation of the
P_{s}
-pseudoalgebras. We shall illustrate our approach by starting with a simpler example.

Example 7.2
.

Let
{({-})}_{\bot}
be the 2-monad on
\mathbf{CAT}
that freely adjoins an initial object. Suppose that we wish to characterise its algebras, but only permit ourselves to apply
{({-})}_{\bot}
to small categories (in preparation for considering the restriction along
J\colon\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
).

Let
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
be a
{({-})}_{\bot}
-algebra (where
A
is not necessarily small). The simplest example of a functor into
A
with small domain is the unique functor
[]_{A}\colon 0\to A
from the empty category. Using the algebra structure, we obtain a functor
([]_{A})^{a}\colon 0_{\bot}=1\to A
, which picks out an object
0_{A}
in
A
.

The next simplest example of a functor into
A
with small domain is a functor
f\colon 1\to A
from the terminal category. Such a functor picks out an object
f(*)
in
A
. Using the algebra structure, we obtain a functor
f^{a}\colon 1_{\bot}=\{0\to 1\}\to A
from the interval category. Such a functor picks out a morphism in
A
. Furthermore, the unit law for the algebra implies that its codomain must be
f(*)
, while the extension law implies that its domain must be
0_{A}
. In other words, the structure of an algebra for
{({-})}_{\bot}
exhibits a specified object
0_{A}
and morphisms therefrom to each object in
A
. Furthermore, a calculation with the algebra axioms shows that every morphism from
0_{A}
is given in this way (see
Lemma

7.8
).

Consequently, it is possible to prove that
{({-})}_{\bot}
-algebras admit initial objects, by extending only functors with domain
0
and
1
. In the other direction, an easy calculation shows that categories with initial objects admit
{({-})}_{\bot}
-algebra structures. This shows that, for any pseudofunctor
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbf{CAT}
with
{\mathcal{A}}
a full sub-2-category of
\mathbf{CAT}
containing
0
and
1
, the algebras for the
J
-relative pseudomonad
J{({-})}_{\bot}
are locally small categories with initial objects.

This example suggests it should be possible to characterise the
P
-pseudoalgebras by using their extension operators to construct colimiting cocones for any functor thereinto with small domain. We will show this is indeed the case in
Theorem

7.14
. In fact, we will work more generally, and consider the relative pseudomonad for the free cocompletion under a class
\Phi
of small colimits, in doing so characterising the pseudoalgebras for several more examples of relative pseudomonads from
[
FGHW18
]
.

Remark 7.3
.

It is perhaps worth saying a few words regarding the relationship between the presheaf construction and the small presheaf construction. Since the presheaf construction may be obtained from the small presheaf construction by restricting along
\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
, one might suppose the small presheaf construction to be the more fundamental of the two. However, as will be justified in
Theorem

7.14
, the small presheaf construction may also be obtained from the presheaf construction. Thus the two constructions are, in an appropriate sense, equivalent. That the presheaf construction is the simpler of the two constructions suggests that it is the presheaf construction that deserves to be viewed as the fundamental construction, and the small presheaf construction as derived therefrom. This aligns with the perspective in
[
ACU15
,
Ark22
]
that relative monads may be fruitfully considered as presentations of monads on their codomain. As further evidence to support this perspective, it is really the Kleisli bicategory of
P
(i.e. the bicategory of small categories and distributors) rather than of
P_{s}
(whose Kleisli bicategory comprises the locally small categories and
small
distributors) that is of importance in category theory.

Remark 7.4
.

It was proven abstractly in
[
KL00
, Theorem 7.1]
that the small presheaf construction may be strictified, in the sense that there is a 2-monad on
\mathbf{CAT}
whose 2-category of (strict) algebras and pseudomorphisms is biequivalent to
\mathrm{PsAlg}(P_{s})
over
\mathbf{CAT}
. By precomposing the inclusion
\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
, the presheaf construction
P
may therefore similarly be strictified into a relative 2-monad. However, in doing so we lose the simplicity of the concrete description of the presheaf construction. An explicit description of the free strict cocompletion is given in
[
BPG21
]
; the reader will note that it is significantly more complicated. Furthermore, such a description does not simplify the description of the algebras.

7.1.
Colimits in pseudoalgebras

We aim to carry out the construction of
Example

7.2
for more general classes of colimits. In fact, we will show that the argument therein applies under relatively few assumptions on the relative pseudomonad in question. The construction is essentially a study of cocones in pseudoalgebras. We recall in
Lemma

7.7
a description of cocones that will be convenient for what follows. This in turn requires a preparatory definition.

Definition 7.5
.

Denote by
j_{D}\colon D\hookrightarrow D_{\top}
the free category with a terminal object on
D
. Explicitly,

|D_{\top}|\mathrel{:=}|D|+\{\top\}

\displaystyle D_{\top}(\top,\top)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\{1_{\top}\}

\displaystyle D_{\top}(d,\top)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\{{\langle\rangle}_{d}\}

\displaystyle D_{\top}(d,d^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}D(d,d^{\prime})

\displaystyle D_{\top}(\top,d)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\varnothing

where
d,d^{\prime}\in|D|
. For each functor
f\colon D\to A
whose codomain has a chosen terminal object, denote by
f^{\top}\colon D_{\top}\to A
the unique terminal object-preserving functor rendering the following diagram commutative.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We will need a simple property of this construction.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
g\colon A_{\top}\to B
be a functor preserving the terminal object. Given any functor
f\colon A_{\top}\to B
and a natural transformation
\phi\colon fj_{A}\Rightarrow gj_{A}
as follows, there is a unique natural transformation
\tau_{\phi}\colon f\Rightarrow g
such that
\phi=\tau_{\phi}j_{A}
.

Proof.

The components of
\tau_{\phi}
on objects in
A
is determined by
\phi
. The component at the terminal object is determined by the unique morphism into the terminal object in
B
. Naturality is trivially satisfied.
∎

We use the
{({-})}_{\top}
construction to characterise cocones under
D
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
f\colon D\to A
be a functor with small domain. Cocones under
f
are in natural bijection with functors
D_{\top}\to A
whose restriction along
j_{D}\colon D\to D_{\top}
is
f
, and their morphisms are in bijection with natural transformations that restrict to
1_{f}
.

Proof.

The nadir of the cocone is given by evaluating a functor at the terminal object, while each leg is given by evaluating the functor at the unique morphism from the terminal object.
∎

The key lemma of this section is the following, which gives sufficient conditions for a pseudoalgebra for a relative pseudomonad with codomain
\mathbf{CAT}
to admit a colimit of a given functor.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a full sub-2-category of
\mathbf{CAT}
, let
J
be the inclusion
{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
, and let
T
be a
J
-relative pseudomonad. Further suppose that
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is a
T
-pseudoalgebra and that
D\in{\mathcal{A}}
is a category such that

(7.8.(1))

D_{\top}\in{\mathcal{A}}
;

(7.8.(2))

T(D)
admits a terminal object;

(7.8.(3))

i_{D_{\top}}\colon J(D_{\top})\to T(D_{\top})
preserves the terminal object.

Then any functor
f\colon J(D)\to A
admits a colimit.

Proof.

First, observe that we have a composite functor
f^{a}\circ(i_{D})^{\top}\colon J(D)_{\top}\to A
, which by
Lemma

7.7
exhibits a cocone under
f^{a}\circ i_{D}
. We will show that this cocone is colimiting: this suffices to establish the result, since
f^{a}\circ i_{D}\cong f
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J(D)_{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J(D)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{D})^{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{J(D)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

We begin by establishing weak initiality. Let
k\colon J(D)_{\top}\to A
be a cocone under
f^{a}\circ i_{D}
(so that
k\circ j_{J(D)}=f^{a}i_{D}
). We form a morphism of cocones

\kappa\colon f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}\to k

as the following composite functor.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}i_{D})^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{a}i_{D_{\top}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(kj_{J(D)})^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k^{a}i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)})^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{a}(i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)})^{*}(i_{D})^{\top}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{a}_{f}^{-1})^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{k}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{a}_{k}j_{J(D)})^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{k,i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)}}(i_{D})^{\top}}

The unlabelled 2-cell on the right-hand side is defined by applying
Lemma

7.6
to the 2-cell below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J(D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J(D)_{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J(D)_{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(D_{\top})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{J(D)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{J(D)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{D}}^{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{-1}_{i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{D_{\top}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

To see verify that
\kappa
is a morphism of cocones we must show
\kappa\circ j_{J(D)}
is the identity. This follows from commutativity of the following diagram, where the boundary is obtained by precomposing the diagram defining
\kappa
above by
j_{J(D)}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}i_{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}i_{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}i_{D})^{a}i_{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{kj_{J(D)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{a}i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(kj_{J(D)})^{a}i_{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k^{a}i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)})^{a}i_{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{a}(i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)})^{*}i_{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{f}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{a}_{f}^{-1})^{a}i_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{f^{a}i_{D}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{k}^{-1}j_{J(D)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{kj_{J(D)}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{a}_{k}j_{D})^{a}i_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{k^{a}i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{k,i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)}}i_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{a}\eta^{-1}_{i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\tilde{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\tilde{a}}
A
unit.
′

Finally, we show any morphism of cocones must be equal to this one. To this end, first observe that the following diagram in
\mathbf{CAT}[T(D),A]
commutes. Note that the composite
i_{D_{\top}}\circ j_{J(D)}
is well-typed because
J
is injective-on-objects, so
J(D_{\top})=D_{\top}=(JD)_{\top}
. We suppress some of the subscripts below for reasons of space.

(31)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}i_{D})^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}(i_{D})^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}((i_{D})^{\top}j_{J(D)})^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}j_{J(D)})^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}(({i_{D}}^{\top})^{*}i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)})^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}({i_{D}}^{\top})^{*}i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)})^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top})^{a}i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)})^{a}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\hat{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{f,(i^{\top})^{*}ij}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\hat{a}_{f,i^{\top}}ij)^{a}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{f,i^{\top}}(ij)^{*}}
A

A
unit.
′
A
assoc.

Denoting by
t
the terminal object in
T(D)
, which has been assumed to exist, and using that

preserves the terminal object also by assumption, the following diagram commutes in
A
.

(32)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}((i_{D})^{\top})^{*}(i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)})^{*}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top})^{a}(i_{D_{\top}}j_{J(D)})^{*}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}((i_{D})^{\top})^{*}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top})^{a}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}((i_{D})^{\top})^{*}i_{D_{\top}}(\top)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top})^{a}i_{D_{\top}}(\top)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}(\top)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{a}(i^{\top})^{*}({\langle\rangle})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{f,i^{\top}}(ij)^{*}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{a}i^{\top})^{a}({\langle\rangle})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{f,i^{\top}}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{a}\eta_{i^{\top}}^{-1}(\top)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}_{f,i^{\top}}i_{D_{\top}}(\top)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}_{fi^{\top}}^{-1}(\top)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\hat{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{D_{\top}}\text{ preserves terminal obj.}}
A
unit.
′

Evaluating (
31
) at
t\in T(D)
and pasting on top of (
32
), we obtain a commutative diagram of morphisms
(f^{a}i_{D})^{a}(t)\to(f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top})(\top)=f^{a}(t)
in
A
. Tracing the anticlockwise route from top-left (
f^{a}(t)
) to bottom (
f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}(\top)=f^{a}(t)
) in this composite diagram, we obtain a morphism of the form

f^{a}(\cdots)\colon f^{a}(t)\to f^{a}(t)
, which must, the universal property of the terminal object
t\in T(D)
, be equal to the identity.
It follows that
(\tilde{a}^{-1}_{f})^{a}(t)
is equal to the clockwise composite from
(f^{a}i_{D})^{a}(t)
to
(f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top})(\top)
.

Now let
\gamma\colon f^{a}(i_{D})^{\top}\Rightarrow k
be any morphism of cocones under
f^{a}i_{D}
(so that
\gamma\circ j_{J(D)}
is the identity). We must show that the components of
\kappa
and
\gamma
on the terminal object
\top\in J(D)_{\top}
are equal. For this, observe that the following diagram commutes in
A
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}i^{\top}j)^{a}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(kj)^{a}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((f^{a}i^{\top})^{a}ij)^{a}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k^{a}ij)^{a}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}i^{\top})^{a}(ij)^{*}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{a}(ij)^{*}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}i^{\top})^{a}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{a}(t)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{a}i^{\top})^{a}i(\top)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k^{a}i(\top)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{a}i^{\top}(\top)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k(\top)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma j)^{a}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{a}j)^{a}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tilde{a}j)^{a}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma^{a}ij)^{a}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{a}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{a}(ij)^{*}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{a}i^{\top})^{a}({\langle\rangle})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{a}({\langle\rangle})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{a}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{a}i(\top)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}^{-1}(\top)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{a}^{-1}(\top)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma(\top)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\tilde{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\hat{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\gamma^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\gamma^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{nat.\ of }\tilde{a}}

The top morphism
(\gamma j_{J(D)})^{a}(t)
is the identity, since
\gamma
is a cocone. By the calculation above, the composite morphism on the left is equal to
(\tilde{a}^{-1}_{f})^{a}(t)
.
It follows that
\gamma(\top)
is equal to the composite morphism on the right, precomposed with
(\tilde{a_{f}}^{-1})^{a}(t)
. But this is exactly the definition of
\kappa(\top)
, as required.
∎

The fundamental assumption of
Lemma

7.8
is the existence of terminal objects in free pseudoalgebras. The following gives a useful sufficient condition therefor.

Lemma 7.9
.

A dense functor admits a colimit if and only if its codomain admits a terminal object, in which case the terminal object exhibits the colimit.

Proof.

Let
j\colon A\to B
be a functor. The colimit of
j
, if it exists, is given by the left extension
{\langle\rangle}_{A}\mathbin{\rhd}j
of
j
along the unique functor
{\langle\rangle}_{A}\colon A\to 1

[
Bor94
, Proposition 3.7.5]
. The terminal object of
B
, if it exists, is given by the colimit of
1_{B}

[
Bor94
, Corollary 2.11.6]
, hence by the left extension
{\langle\rangle}_{B}\mathbin{\rhd}1_{B}
of
1_{B}
along
{\langle\rangle}_{B}\colon B\to 1
. We have the following natural isomorphisms, using density of
j
.

{\langle\rangle}_{A}\mathbin{\rhd}j\cong({\langle\rangle}_{B}j)\mathbin{\rhd}j\cong{\langle\rangle}_{B}\mathbin{\rhd}(j\mathbin{\rhd}j)\cong{\langle\rangle}_{B}\mathbin{\rhd}1_{B}\qed

7.2.
Free
\Phi
-cocompletions

We now use the techniques of the previous subsection to prove the main result of this section, which is a characterisation of the bicategories of pseudoalgebras for relative pseudomonads arising from presheaf constructions.

Definition 7.10
.

Let
\Phi
be a class of small categories. Denote by
\Phi\textup{-}\mathbf{COC}
the 2-category whose objects are locally small categories equipped with a choice of
\Phi
-colimits: specifically, an object is a locally small category
A
along with, for each category
D\in\Phi
and functor
f\colon D\to A
, a cocone under
f
that is colimiting. A 1-cell is a functor that sends the colimiting cocones to colimiting cocones (not necessarily the chosen ones). The 2-cells are natural transformations.

Example 7.11
.

Let
\Phi
be a class of small categories.
Denote by
J\colon\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
the inclusion of the 2-category of small categories into the 2-category of locally small categories. The
\Phi
-cocompletion
sends a small category
A
to the smallest full subcategory of
[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
closed under representables and
\Phi
-colimits. This exhibits a left
J
-relative pseudoadjoint
\overline{\Phi}\colon\mathbf{Cat}\to\Phi\textup{-}\mathbf{COC}
to the forgetful 2-functor
U\colon\Phi\textup{-}\mathbf{COC}\to\mathbf{CAT}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi\textup{-}\mathbf{COC}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CAT}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The unit
\phi_{A}\colon A\to\overline{\Phi}(A)
is given by the Yoneda embedding. For each small category
X
and
\Phi
-cocomplete locally small category
A
, the functor

\mathbf{CAT}[X,U(A)]\xleftarrow{U{({-})}\circ\phi_{A}}\Phi\textup{-}\mathbf{COC}[\overline{\Phi}(X),A]

admits a left adjoint equivalence, given by left extension along
\phi_{A}

[
Kel82
, Theorem 5.35]
. We denote the induced
J
-relative pseudomonad by
\overline{\Phi}
.

In particular, taking
\Phi
to be the class of small categories,
\overline{\Phi}
is simply the presheaf construction
P
of
Example

2.8
. We may now instantiate
Lemma

7.8
to the case of relative pseudomonads exhibiting free cocompletions under classes of colimits.

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
\Phi\subseteq\Phi^{\prime}
be classes of small categories for which
D\in\Phi
implies
D_{\top}\in\Phi^{\prime}
. Denote by
J\colon\Phi^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
the full sub-2-category inclusion of the class
\Phi^{\prime}
. Every
\overline{\Phi}J
-pseudoalgebra is
\Phi
-cocomplete, and every pseudomorphism is
\Phi
-cocontinuous.

Proof.

Let
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
be a
\overline{\Phi}J
-pseudoalgebra and let
f\colon J(D)\to A
be a functor. The functor
\phi_{D}\colon J(D)\to\overline{\Phi}(D)
is dense and admits a colimit since
D\in\Phi
, so
\overline{\Phi}(D)
admits a terminal object by
Lemma

7.9
. Hence, since the Yoneda embedding preserves limits,
Lemma

7.8
implies that
f
admits a colimit.

Now let
(g,\overline{g})\colon(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})\to(B,{-}^{b},\hat{b},\tilde{b})
be a pseudomorphism of
\overline{\Phi}J
-pseudoalgebras. Given a small diagram
f\colon D\to A
, we have a natural transformation

\overline{h}_{f}\colon(hf)^{b}{\phi_{B}}^{\top}\Rightarrow hf^{a}{\phi_{A}}^{\top}

exhibiting a morphism of cocones. By definition of the colimiting cocones in
A
and
B
described by
Lemma

7.8
, this morphism restricts at the nadirs to a morphism
\colim fd\Rightarrow f\colim d
, which is precisely the canonical morphism of cocones whose invertibility exhibits cocontinuity.
∎

Consequently, we observe that every pseudoalgebra for a free cocompletion is lax-idempotent. This significantly strengthens
[
FGHW18
, Example 5.4]
, which establishes lax-idempotence for free
P
-pseudoalgebras (recalling
Proposition

5.4
).

Corollary 7.13
.

Under the same assumptions as
Proposition

7.12
, every
\overline{\Phi}J
-pseudoalgebra is lax-idempotent.

Proof.

Let
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
be a
\overline{\Phi}J
-pseudoalgebra. Let
f\colon D\to A
be a diagram for which
D\in\Phi
. By
Propositions

7.12
and
3.20
,
f^{a}\colon\overline{\Phi}(D)\to A
is
\Phi
-cocontinuous, and
f^{a}\phi_{A}
is isomorphic to
f
. Since
\overline{\Phi}(D)
is the free
\Phi
-cocompletion of
D
,
f^{a}
is therefore the left extension of
f
along
\phi_{D}
by
[
Kel82
, Theorem 5.35]
. Consequently, by
Lemma

5.2
,
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is lax-idempotent.
∎

We now have everything we need in place to prove the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 7.14
.

The following relative pseudoadjunction is relatively pseudomonadic, exhibiting
\mathrm{PsAlg}(\overline{\Phi})
as biequivalent to
\Phi\textup{-}\mathbf{COC}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi\textup{-}\mathbf{COC}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CAT}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

More generally, this is true with respect to any
J
satisfying the assumptions of
Proposition

7.12
.

Proof.

By definition (
Example

7.11
), the relative pseudoadjunction is a resolution of
\overline{\Phi}
in the sense of
Definition

6.1
. Consequently, by
Theorem

6.10
, there is a unique comparison pseudofunctor
{!_{\Phi}}\colon\Phi\textup{-}\mathbf{COC}\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(\overline{\Phi})
commuting with the left and right pseudoadjoints. It suffices to show that the comparison is essentially surjective and essentially fully faithful.

First, since every
\overline{\Phi}
-pseudoalgebra
(A,{-}^{a},\hat{a},\tilde{a})
is
\Phi
-cocomplete by
Proposition

7.12
, the category
A
is
\Phi
-cocomplete, and thus induces a
\overline{\Phi}
-pseudoalgebra
!_{\Phi}(A)
, which is necessarily lax-idempotent by
Corollary

7.13
. Since any two lax-idempotent pseudoalgebra structures on the same object are unique by
Corollary

5.10
, the comparison pseudofunctor is consequently essentially surjective.

Second, observe that
\Phi\textup{-}\mathbf{COC}\to\mathbf{CAT}
is locally fully faithful, with its image being the
\Phi
-cocontinuous
functors and all natural transformations. By
Corollary

5.8
, since every
\overline{\Phi}
-pseudoalgebra is lax-idempotent,
\mathrm{PsAlg}(\overline{\Phi})\to\mathbf{CAT}
is also locally fully faithful, and its 1-cells are
\Phi
-cocontinuous functors by
Proposition

7.12
. Thus the comparison pseudofunctor is locally an equivalence.
∎

While we are primarily interested in
Theorem

7.14
when
J\colon\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
is the inclusion of small categories, it is worth emphasising that the strongest result is obtained taking
\Phi^{\prime}
to be the closure of the class
\Phi
under
{({-})}_{\top}
. In general, the smaller the domain of a relative pseudomonad, the more pseudoalgebras there are, because in that case the pseudoalgebras need to admit extensions with respect to fewer 1-cells. The fact that, in the situation of
Theorem

7.14
, restricting the domain to
\Phi^{\prime}
does not change the pseudoalgebras means, intuitively, that the pseudoalgebra structure is determined by ‘probing’ merely with objects in
\Phi^{\prime}
.

Example 7.15
.

•

Let
\Phi
be the class of small categories. Then
\overline{\Phi}
is the presheaf construction relative pseudomonad of
Example

2.8
, and its pseudoalgebras are precisely the locally small categories admitting small colimits. The free
\overline{\Phi}
-pseudoalgebras are precisely the presheaf categories.

•

Let
\Phi
be the class of filtered categories. Then
\overline{\Phi}
is the Ind-completion relative pseudomonad
[
FGHW18
, Example 3.9]
, and its pseudoalgebras are precisely the locally small categories admitting filtered colimits. The free
\overline{\Phi}
-pseudoalgebras are precisely the finitely accessible categories. Similar remarks apply taking
\Phi
to be the class of
\kappa
-filtered categories, for a regular cardinal
\kappa
.

•

Let
\Phi
be the class of sifted categories. Then
\overline{\Phi}
is the Sind-completion relative pseudomonad, and its pseudoalgebras are precisely the locally small categories admitting sifted colimits. The free
\overline{\Phi}
-pseudoalgebras are precisely the generalised varieties in the sense of
[
AR01
]
.

•

Let
\Phi
be the class of small discrete categories. Then
\overline{\Phi}
is the Fam-completion relative pseudomonad, and its pseudoalgebras are precisely the locally small categories admitting coproducts. This resolves a question in
[
Lew20
, Chapter 4, Footnote 39]
.

•

Let
\Phi
be the singleton class
\{\varnothing\}
. Then
\overline{\Phi}
freely adjoins an initial object, and we recover
Example

7.2
.

For completeness, we make explicit the relationship between the relative pseudomonad of
Example

7.11
exhibiting the free
\Phi
-cocompletion of
small
categories and pseudomonad exhibiting the free
\Phi
-cocompletion of
locally small
categories.

Definition 7.16
.

Let
\Phi
be a class of small categories. The assignment of a locally small category
A
to the smallest full subcategory of
[A{}^{\mathrm{op}},{\mathbf{Set}}]
closed under representables and
\Phi
-colimits defines a pseudomonad
\overline{\Phi}_{s}
on the 2-category
\mathbf{CAT}
of locally small categories
[
Kel82
, Theorem 5.35]
.

Let
J\colon\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
be the inclusion of small categories. For each class
\Phi
of small categories, we have
\overline{\Phi}=\overline{\Phi}_{s}J
, which induces a canonical comparison pseudofunctor
!_{J}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}(\overline{\Phi}_{s})\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(\overline{\Phi})
by
Corollary

6.14
.

Corollary 7.17
.

The canonical pseudofunctor
!_{J}\colon\mathrm{PsAlg}(\overline{\Phi}_{s})\to\mathrm{PsAlg}(\overline{\Phi})
is a biequivalence. More generally, this is true with respect to any
J
satisfying the assumptions of
Proposition

7.12
.

Proof.

As described in the introduction to the section,
\mathrm{PsAlg}(\overline{\Phi}_{s})
is concretely biequivalent to
\Phi\textup{-}\mathbf{COC}
, and this biequivalence trivially factors through
!_{J}
.
∎

Consequently, following the terminology of
[
Ark22
, Definition 5.4.2]
, the
\Phi
-cocompletion
\overline{\Phi}_{s}
is
J
-ary
, in that restriction along
J
preserves the 2-categories of pseudoalgebras and pseudomorphisms.

Remark 7.18
.

It is likely that a more abstract proof of
Theorem

7.14
along the lines of
[
ACU15
, §4.4]
or
[
Ark22
, §5.4]
is possible, since the inclusion 2-functor
\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
is well-behaved in a two-dimensional sense. We defer such an investigation to future work.

Remark 7.19
.

We may also characterise the 2-categories of
\overline{\Phi}
-pseudoalgebras and strict/lax morphisms. The strict morphisms are those that send chosen colimiting cocones to chosen colimiting cocones. By
Theorem

5.7
, lax morphisms are simply arbitrary functors, since
\overline{\Phi}
-pseudoalgebras are lax-idempotent by
Corollary

7.13
. A characterisation of the colax morphisms would likely require a resolution to
Remark

5.24
.

Remark 7.20
.

Our proof strategy for
Theorem

7.14
relies crucially upon
Lemma

7.8
, which uses the fact that cocones under functors
f\colon D\to A
may be represented by functors
D_{\top}\to A
. This approach is effective for ordinary categories, and likely also for internal categories. However, it is not clear how to extend this technique to capture cocompleteness for enriched categories. Indeed, it seems likely that, although free cocompletions under classes
\Phi
of enriched weights do induce lax-idempotent relative pseudomonads, their algebras are not
\Phi
-cocomplete in general. We leave the exploration of such questions to future work.

7.3.
Applications of coherence

We conclude this section by giving some promised applications of the coherence theorem of
Corollary

6.15
to
\Phi
-cocompletion relative pseudomonads. We first recall an alternative presentation of free cocompletions in terms of discrete fibrations rather than presheaves.

Example 7.21

(Discrete fibration construction)
.

Let
X
be a small category. For each presheaf
p\colon X{}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}
, the projection from its category of elements
\pi\colon{\mathrm{El}}(p)\to X
is a discrete fibration over
X
. This assignment is functorial, and defines an adjoint equivalence between the category
P(X)
of presheaves on
X
and natural transformations, and the category
Q(X)
of discrete fibrations over
X
and commutative triangles. Furthermore, the following triangle commutes, where
X/{({-})}
is the functor sending each object
x\in X
to the slice category
X/x
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{El}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X/{({-})}}

Consequently, by
Example

2.3
, the discrete fibration construction defines a left relative pseudoadjoint to the forgetful 2-functor
U\colon\mathbf{COC}\to\mathbf{CAT}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{COC}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CAT}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

This in turn defines a
J
-relative pseudomonad
Q
that is equivalent, in a suitable sense
12
12

12

We leave it to the reader to formulate the appropriate definitions of morphism and transformation of relative pseudomonads necessary to make this statement precise; it will not be necessary for what follows.
, to the presheaf construction.

Recall from
Example

2.12
that the bicategory
{\mathbf{Dist}}
of categories, distributors, and natural transformations is defined to be the Kleisli bicategory
\mathrm{Kl}(P)
associated to the presheaf construction (
Example

2.8
). This motivates consideration of the Kleisli bicategory associated to the discrete fibration construction.

Definition 7.22
.

The bicategory
{\mathbf{TSDFib}}
of categories, two-sided discrete fibrations, and span morphisms is defined to be the Kleisli bicategory
\mathrm{Kl}(Q)
associated to the discrete fibration construction (
Example

7.21
).

We note that in the above definition that a 1-cell in
\mathrm{Kl}(Q)
is not exactly the usual presentation of a two-sided discrete fibration in terms of a span of categories satisfying a fibration property
[
Str80
]
, but the data is elementarily equivalent, so it is harmless to identify the notions.

The following theorem appears to be folklore; while this intuition is already present in
[
Str80
]
, we were unable to find a proof in the literature, but it follows easily using the techniques developed in
Section

6
.

Theorem 7.23
.

{\mathbf{Dist}}
is biequivalent to
{\mathbf{TSDFib}}
.

Proof.

P
and
D
both exhibit left pseudoadjoints to
U\colon\mathbf{COC}\to\mathbf{CAT}
. By
Corollary

6.7
, their Kleisli bicategories are given by taking the full image factorisations of
P
and
Q
respectively, and this process evidently preserves biequivalence.
∎

We now give a number of examples of the coherence theorem for relative pseudomonads. None are new, but have typically been established on a case-by-case basis; our work shows that they are consequences of a single phenomenon.

Example 7.24
.

Corollary

6.15
, in combination with
Corollary

6.13
, gives various well-known coherence theorems as consequences (cf.
[
Lac10
, §1.5]
). In each of the following, we denote by
J\colon{\mathbf{Set}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
the inclusion of sets viewed as discrete categories.

(7.24.(1))

From
Theorem

7.14
, the 2-category of pseudoalgebras for the presheaf construction is the 2-category of locally small categories with a choice of small colimits. Thus
Corollary

6.15
exhibits a biequivalence between the bicategory
{\mathbf{Dist}}
of distributors and the full sub-2-category
\mathbf{Psh}
of
\mathbf{COC}
spanned by the presheaf categories. This provides a proof of the claim in
[
CW96
, Proposition 4.2.4]
. Similarly, there is a biequivalence between the bicategory
{\mathbf{TSDFib}}
of two-sided discrete fibrations and the full sub-2-category
{\mathbf{DFib}}
of
\mathbf{COC}
spanned by categories of discrete fibrations.

(7.24.(2))

Restricting each of
{\mathbf{Dist}}
,
{\mathbf{TSDFib}}
,
\mathbf{Psh}
, and
{\mathbf{DFib}}
along
J
, we observe that the following bicategories are biequivalent to one another.

(7.24.(1).(a))

The bicategory
\mathrm{Kl}(PJ)\mathrel{:=}\mathbf{Mat}
of sets and matrices.

(7.24.(2).(b))

The bicategory
\mathrm{Kl}(QJ)\mathrel{:=}\mathbf{Span}
of sets and spans.

(7.24.(3).(c))

The 2-category
\mathrm{Im}(F_{PJ})
of indexed sets.

(7.24.(4).(d))

The 2-category
\mathrm{Im}(F_{QJ})
of slice categories.

(7.24.(3))

Let
{\mathbf{Fam}}
be the pseudomonad for free coproduct cocompletion. Since the free coproduct completion of a discrete category is equivalent to its free colimit completion,
{\mathbf{Fam}}\circ J
is biequivalent to
PJ
. Consequently,
\mathrm{Kl}({\mathbf{Fam}}\circ J)
, which is biequivalent to
\mathrm{Im}(F_{{\mathbf{Fam}}\circ J})
, is biequivalent to the bicategory of sets and spans, recovering
[
LS02
, §A]
.

(7.24.(4))

Let
T
be the
J
-relative 2-monad freely adjoining an initial object. Then
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
has as objects sets, as 1-cells partial functions, and has a 2-cell from
f
to
g
if and only if for all
x
, either
f(x)
is undefined or
f(x)=g(x)
. It is biequivalent to the 2-category of pointed sets, point-preserving functions, in which there is a 2-cell from
f
to
g
if and only if for all
x
, either
f(x)
is the point or
f(x)=g(x)
.

Example 7.25
.

From
Corollary

6.7
, it also follows that the bicategory of distributors is biequivalent to the full image of the left pseudoadjoint of any resolution of the presheaf construction. This recovers, for instance, the perspective of
[
GJ17
, §1.3]
, who define the bicategory of distributors as the full image of the left pseudoadjoint of the forgetful pseudofunctor
U\colon\mathbf{LP}_{L}\to\mathbf{CAT}
from the 2-category
\mathbf{LP}_{L}
of locally presentable categories, left-adjoint functors, and natural transformations.

8.
Cocompletion-relative monads as cocontinuous monads

We conclude with an application of the theory of relative pseudomonads to the theory of relative monads, which makes use of two of our main theorems: the full faithfulness of the embedding of
\mathrm{Kl}(T)
in
\mathrm{PsAlg}(T)
, and the characterisation of the pseudoalgebras for relative pseudomonads arising from free cocompletions.

For any class
\Phi
of small categories, and denoting by
J\colon\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAT}
the full sub-2-category inclusion of small categories,
Theorem

7.14
establishes that there exists a
J
-relative pseudomonad
\overline{\Phi}
whose pseudoalgebras are precisely the locally small categories admitting
\Phi
-colimits. Now, let
X
be a small category and consider its free cocompletion
\phi_{X}\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{\Phi}(X)
under
\Phi
-colimits. There is a monoidal hom-category
\mathrm{Kl}(\overline{\Phi})(X,X)
, whose underlying category is given by
\mathbf{CAT}[X,\overline{\Phi}(X)]
. It was proven in
[
AM24
, Theorem 4.7]
that the category
\mathbf{CAT}[X,\overline{\Phi}(X)]
is also equipped with skew-multicategory structure
\mathbf{CAT}[\phi_{X}]
, in which the monoids are precisely the
\phi_{X}
-relative monads
[
AM24
, Theorem 4.16]
. Is it natural to wonder whether these two structures are related: the following lemma shows that they coincide; essentially the same observation appears in
[
Szl17
, 215]
, albeit without the two-dimensional perspective.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
\Phi
be a class of small categories, and let
X
be a small category. The skew-multicategory
\mathbf{CAT}[\phi_{X}]
is represented by the monoidal hom-category
\mathrm{Kl}(\overline{\Phi})[X,X]
.

Proof.

First, observe that, since
\phi_{X}\colon X\to\overline{\Phi}(X)
is a cocompletion, it satisfies conditions (1 – 3) of
[
AM24
, Theorem 4.29]
, so that the skew-multicategory
\mathbf{CAT}[\phi_{X}]
is represented by
some
monoidal category. We show it is monoidally equivalent to
\mathrm{Kl}(\overline{\Phi})[X,X]
. The underlying categories are isomorphic. The monoidal unit in both cases is given by
\phi_{X}
. The tensor product of
f\colon X\to\overline{\Phi}(X)
and
g\colon A\to\overline{\Phi}(X)
in the former is given by
f\mathbin{;}(\phi_{X}\mathbin{{\rhd}\mathclap{\mspace{-17.5mu}\cdot}}g)
, the composition of
f
with the (pointwise) left extension of
g
along
\phi_{X}
. This is, by definition, the composition in the Kleisli bicategory
[
FGHW18
, Theorem 4.1]
.
∎

For the main theorem of this section, we shall need a preparatory lemma.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
J\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a pseudofunctor and let
T
be a lax-idempotent
J
-relative pseudomonad. Then, for each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
, the functor
(V_{T})_{X,X}\colon\mathrm{Kl}(T)[X,X]\to{\mathcal{E}}[TX,TX]
admits a right adjoint with invertible counit, and this adjunction lifts to the 2-category of monoidal categories, lax monoidal functors, and monoidal natural transformations.

Proof.

Recall from
Theorem

2.13
that
(V_{T})_{X,X}
is given by
{({-})}^{*}_{X,Y}\colon{\mathcal{E}}[JX,TY]\to{\mathcal{E}}[TX,TX]
, which admits a right adjoint with invertible counit when
T
is lax-idempotent by
Proposition

5.4
.
That
(V_{T})_{X,X}
is pseudo monoidal follows from pseudofunctoriality of
V_{T}
, whence the adjunction lifts to monoidal categories by doctrinal adjunction (
Proposition

4.2
).
∎

The first half of the following theorem was proven in
[
ACU15
, Theorem 4.8]
via explicit calculations; the second half was proven in
[
Ark22
, Proposition 3.2.5]
. We give a new, more conceptual proof, of both.

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
\Phi
be a class of small categories, and let
A
be a small category. Left extension along
\phi_{X}\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{\Phi}(X)
, the free cocompletion of
X
under
\Phi
-colimits, defines a coreflection

(33)

\mathbf{RMnd}(\phi_{X}\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{\Phi}(X))\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Mnd}(\overline{\Phi}(X))

which restricts to an equivalence of categories

(34)

\mathbf{RMnd}(\phi_{X}\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{\Phi}(X))\simeq\mathbf{Mnd}_{\Phi}(\overline{\Phi}(X))

between the category of
\phi_{X}
-relative monads, and the category of
\Phi
-cocontinuous monads on
\overline{\Phi}(X)
. Furthermore, this equivalence respects the process of taking algebras, in that the following diagram of categories commutes up to natural isomorphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{RMnd}(\phi_{X}\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{\Phi}(X)){}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Mnd}_{\Phi}(\overline{\Phi}(X)){}^{\mathrm{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{CAT}/\overline{\Phi}(X)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Alg}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Alg}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Proof.

Since
\Phi
is a lax-idempotent pseudomonad, there there is a lax monoidal coreflection

\mathrm{Kl}(\overline{\Phi})[X,X]\rightleftarrows\mathbf{CAT}[\overline{\Phi}(X),\overline{\Phi}(X)]

by
Lemma

8.2
. The coreflection (
33
) then follows by taking monoids on both sides, which gives relative monads on the left-hand side by
Lemma

8.1
. The equivalence (
34
) follows by restricting from
\mathbf{CAT}[\overline{\Phi}(X),\overline{\Phi}(X)]
to
\mathrm{PsAlg}(\overline{\Phi})[\overline{\Phi}(X),\overline{\Phi}(X)]
on the right-hand side, using
Corollary

6.11
. That this equivalence commutes with the process of taking algebras follows from the relative monadicity theorem
[
AM25
, Example 4.8]
.
∎

Example 8.4
.

Let
\kappa
be a regular cardinal, and let
X
be a small category. Take
\Phi
to be the class of
\kappa
-filtered categories. Then
Theorem

8.3
gives an equivalence between monads relative to the cocompletion of
X
under
\kappa
-filtered colimits, and
\kappa
-accessible monads (i.e. monads whose underlying endofunctor preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits) on
\rm{Ind}_{\kappa}(X)
. For instance, the category of monads relative to
\mathbf{FinSet}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{Set}}
, the inclusion of finite sets into sets, is equivalent to the category of finitary monads on
{\mathbf{Set}}
.
